### Visual: the conversion machine, and where each stage can fail

```text
   STAGE 1   THE PAPER CLAIM
             an agent obtains a signed treaty of cession or protection
             from an African ruler; the text is European, the language
             is European, the translation is by an interested party
             FAILURE MODE: the signatory has no authority to bind the
             society, or has agreed to something the text does not say
                                  |
   STAGE 2   THE DIPLOMATIC CLAIM
             the treaty is notified to rival powers; a sphere of
             influence is agreed BETWEEN EUROPEANS; a boundary is
             described in a bilateral convention
             FAILURE MODE: the line runs through territory nobody has
             visited, described by latitude, longitude or a river
                                  |
   STAGE 3   THE OCCUPATION
             a station is founded; a company is chartered; a column
             marches; a garrison of locally recruited soldiers under
             European officers is established
             FAILURE MODE: resistance; disease; cost; the company goes
             bankrupt and the state must take over
                                  |
   STAGE 4   THE ADMINISTRATION
             taxation in cash or labour; recognised or appointed chiefs;
             a district officer; courts; a railway to the coast
             FAILURE MODE: revolt against taxation and labour demands
             -- which is what Maji Maji, 1905-07, actually was
                                  |
   STAGE 5   THE BOUNDARY COMMISSION
             surveyors walk the line agreed in Europe and mark it
             RESULT: boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political
             realities
   =========================================================================
   THE MAP OF 1914 RECORDS STAGE 2.
   IT DOES NOT RECORD STAGE 4.
   Large areas shown as colonies were not yet administered at all.
```

*The five stages are the answer to a question examiners like and candidates rarely handle: what does it actually mean to say a territory was "taken"? Taking is not one act but five, performed by different people over decades, and each stage has its own failure mode. The distinction between stage 2 and stage 4 is also the single best defence against over-reading the 90-per-cent figure of Lesson 1.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

A treaty signed in a village on the upper reaches of a river in 1885 is a piece of paper. It does not collect taxes, build a road, or prevent a rival's agent from obtaining a different piece of paper from a neighbouring ruler the following month. Between that paper and a functioning colonial district lies an enormous amount of work, most of it violent, expensive and slow.

The partition is usually taught as if it ended in 1885 or 1890. In fact the map was drawn quickly and the ground was taken slowly. Conquest campaigns continued through the 1890s and into the twentieth century; some regions were not brought under administration until years after their allocation on a European map; and the largest single African military defeat of a European power occurred in 1896, eleven years after the General Act.

This lesson is about that conversion. It is also, incidentally, the lesson that supplies the material for a question on African resistance, because resistance is what conversion ran into.

### 2. The four instruments

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four instruments did the work, and each has an analysable defect.

**The treaty form.** ✅ European agents carried printed or standardised treaty forms, in a European language, into which the name of a ruler and a territory could be entered; these documents typically conveyed cession of sovereignty or acceptance of protection, and were explained through interpreters who were frequently employees of the claimant. ⚠️ Three defects follow and each is examinable. First, capacity: in decentralised societies there was frequently nobody with authority to alienate collective rights, so the signature bound nothing - ✅ which connects directly to the recorded finding that colonial administration afterwards **recognised and sometimes invented chiefs**. Second, meaning: a ruler might understand an agreement of friendship, alliance or trade where the European text recorded cession of sovereignty. Third, function: the treaty's real audience was **European**, not African - its purpose was to be shown to a rival government as evidence of priority. ⚠️ That third point is the one to carry, because it explains why so many treaties were obtained so quickly and why their African content mattered so little to the men obtaining them.

**The chartered company.** ✅ The historiographical companion records chartered or concessionary control as a distinct mechanism in which **private and state interests mixed** and **profit and sovereignty often overlapped**. ✅ The principal instances in this period include the **Royal Niger Company**, chartered in **1886** in the Niger territories; the **Imperial British East Africa Company**, chartered in **1888**; the **British South Africa Company**, chartered in **1889** and associated with **Cecil Rhodes**; and the **German East Africa Company**, associated with **Carl Peters**. ⚠️ The model's logic is that of empire on credit: the state obtains the claim and the shareholders carry the cost and the risk. ⚠️ Its characteristic outcome is also consistent - most such companies proved unable to bear the cost of administration, and their territories passed to the state, which is the drift predicted in Lesson 3.

**The column.** ✅ Military conversion was normally carried out by small forces of **African soldiers recruited locally or from other regions, under European officers**, equipped with modern firearms and, increasingly, machine guns, and supplied by porters. ⚠️ Two consequences are worth stating. First, the conquest of Africa was carried out very largely **by Africans**, in European service - a fact that is uncomfortable for both a simple European-versus-African narrative and for a narrative of uniform African resistance, and which is why Lesson 15 treats soldiering as a form of agency rather than as an embarrassment. Second, the smallness of these forces is itself the argument of Lesson 7: it is firepower, not numbers, that made such columns effective.

**The boundary commission.** ✅ Frontiers agreed in European conventions were described by geographical coordinates, by watersheds or by rivers, and were subsequently surveyed and marked on the ground by joint commissions, often years later. ✅ The recorded outcome is unambiguous: **boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities**. ⚠️ The mechanism is worth stating explicitly, because it is more precise than "Europeans drew arbitrary lines": a line agreed between two European governments, describing territory neither had visited, using coordinates or physical features chosen for their availability on a map, will intersect political and social realities at random. Arbitrariness here is not malice but method.

### 3. The six powers, and what each held

✅ **Fact:** the standard account records the partition pattern for use in a boundary question, and it should be reproduced accurately.

| Power | Main African zones recorded |
|---|---|
| **Britain** | ✅ Egypt, Sudan, South Africa, Nigeria, East African holdings |
| **France** | ✅ Large parts of West and Equatorial Africa, Algeria, Madagascar |
| **Belgium** | ✅ Congo Free State, later the Congo |
| **Germany** | ✅ Togo, Cameroon, German East Africa, German South West Africa |
| **Portugal** | ✅ Angola, Mozambique |
| **Italy** | ✅ Eritrea, Somalia; failed in Ethiopia at Adwa |

✅ **Fact:** by **1914** only **Ethiopia and Liberia** remained independent.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table is a memorisation aid, and it becomes an analytical instrument only when each holding is attached to a motive and a method. Four observations do that work.

1. **Britain's holdings trace a line of communication**, from Egypt and Sudan down the Nile through East Africa toward South Africa, plus the older West African commercial positions. ⚠️ The pattern confirms the directional role assigned to strategy in Lesson 9.
2. **France's holdings trace a contiguous bloc**, from the Atlantic across the Sahara and Sahel into Equatorial Africa. ⚠️ The logic is territorial consolidation and the prestige of extent, which is why French holdings look enormous on a map and contained a far smaller share of the continent's population and commercial value than their area suggests. ⚠️ That observation - area against value - is worth one clause in any answer that shows a map.
3. **Germany's four territories are scattered and unconnected**, which is exactly what one expects from acquisitions made rapidly for status and bargaining value rather than as a coherent system. ✅ Their subsequent transfer as mandates after the First World War is recorded by the core source's map of Africa and the peace treaties, and belongs to the First World War topic.
4. **Portugal held old coastal positions and converted them into large inland claims**, a case examined in Section 5.

### 4. The three national methods, compared

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** ✅ the record warns against treating imperialism as one uniform model, and the clearest way to honour that warning is to compare the conversion methods rather than the holdings.

| Power | Characteristic instrument of conversion | Characteristic problem |
|---|---|---|
| **Britain** | ⚠️ Chartered companies first, then state administration; rule through recognised African authorities wherever possible; parsimony as policy | ⚠️ Companies could not bear administrative costs, so the state repeatedly had to take over what it had tried to avoid owning |
| **France** | ⚠️ Military conversion by colonial troops and officers, with a strong role for the army and for men on the spot; subsequent centralised administration | ⚠️ Territory acquired by soldiers for military reasons frequently had no economic value, producing a very large and very poor empire |
| **Germany** | ⚠️ Company beginnings followed by direct state administration; extensive use of concessionary grants in some territories | ⚠️ Acute labour and land conflicts in the settler and plantation territories, which produced the two gravest crises of the period: ✅ the Herero and Nama genocide of **1904-08** and ✅ the Maji Maji uprising of **1905-07** |
| **Belgium (Leopold's state, then Belgium)** | ⚠️ Concessionary extraction under a personal sovereignty, then state administration from 1908 | ✅ Sovereignty and profit in one hand, with no metropolitan scrutiny - the pathology examined in Lesson 10 |
| **Portugal** | ⚠️ Historic coastal claims extended inland, with heavy reliance on concession companies and on compelled labour | ⚠️ A weak metropolitan economy holding large territories, which made forced labour central rather than incidental to the colonial system |
| **Italy** | ⚠️ State-led acquisition for status, with limited resources | ✅ Failure in Ethiopia at Adwa, and possession of two of the least rewarding territories on the continent |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison yields a generalisation worth stating: the **poorer the metropolitan power relative to the territory it claimed, the more it relied on compulsion**, because a state that cannot fund an administration must make the territory pay for its own conquest. ⚠️ That is a structural explanation of colonial coercion which does not depend on national character, and it is markedly stronger than a list of atrocities attributed to particular nationalities.

### 5. Two instructive cases in the execution

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two episodes illustrate the conversion process especially clearly and are worth carrying as named examples.

**Portugal, the claim to the interior, and the collision with a rival's line.** ✅ Portugal, holding Angola on the Atlantic and Mozambique on the Indian Ocean, claimed the territory between them - a claim advanced in the so-called **"rose-coloured map"** - and was compelled to abandon the claim after a **British ultimatum in 1890**, because the intervening territory lay across the projected British route from the Cape northwards. ⚠️ The case demonstrates three things at once: that effective occupation was used against a weaker power's historic claims; that longitudinal and latitudinal ambitions collided in southern-central Africa exactly as they did at Fashoda in the north; and that among Europeans the disputes were settled by ultimatum and treaty rather than by war - the procedural peace noted in Lesson 11.

**The Anglo-German settlement of 1890 and the geometry of exchange.** ✅ An Anglo-German agreement of **1890** settled a series of East African boundary questions and transferred the North Sea island of **Heligoland** from Britain to Germany, with Britain's position in **Zanzibar** recognised. ⚠️ The case is the clearest single illustration of the observation in Lesson 5 that African territory functioned as diplomatic currency: a European island was exchanged against East African boundaries, and neither party consulted anybody in East Africa about it. ⚠️ It is also a useful corrective to the assumption that the partition was conducted by force alone; much of it was conducted by trade, between Europeans, in commodities that included other people's countries.

### 6. The gap between the map and the ground

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the lesson's most transferable conclusion and the one most often missing from answers.

✅ **Fact:** the standard account's flow runs: **Berlin rules and effective occupation -> partition of Africa -> resource extraction, resistance and artificial borders**. ⚠️ Notice that resistance appears *after* partition in that sequence, which is exactly right: much of the fighting followed the map rather than preceding it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three consequences follow.

1. **The 90-per-cent figure of 1914 measures claims, not administration.** Substantial areas shown in a colonial colour were not yet effectively administered, some were not administered until well into the twentieth century, and a few were never more than nominally governed.
2. **Colonial "pacification" campaigns are a continuing feature of the period after the partition**, not a preliminary to it. ✅ Maji Maji in **1905-07** is a revolt *under* colonial rule against labour and taxation demands, not a resistance to initial conquest.
3. **The colonial state was thin from the beginning.** ✅ European rule depended on African chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors because the administrative presence was thin - a fact about the whole colonial period whose origin lies in the speed with which claims outran capacity.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Emphasising the gap between claim and administration minimises the partition. However incomplete colonial administration was, sovereignty was extinguished, land was alienated, taxes were levied and armies were defeated, and an argument that stresses the thinness of the colonial state slides toward the old apologetic claim that colonial rule was a light touch that left African societies substantially intact.

**Reply:** The objection identifies a real risk and the reply must meet it directly rather than by qualification. Three points. First, thinness is not lightness: a thin administration with a monopoly of modern force and an absolute demand for revenue is frequently *more* coercive than a dense one, because it lacks the personnel to govern by consent and must therefore govern by exemplary punishment, collective fines and compelled labour. Lesson 17 shows the mechanism and Lesson 18 shows the result. Second, the gap is not offered as a mitigation but as an explanation of colonial *method*: it is precisely because claims outran capacity that rule was exercised through intermediaries, that identities were administratively fixed, and that revolts such as ✅ Maji Maji in 1905-07 occurred under colonial rule against labour and taxation demands rather than at the moment of conquest. Third, the factual point stands independently of its uses: ✅ the recorded flow runs from partition to extraction, resistance and artificial borders, which places resistance after the map, and ✅ only Ethiopia and Liberia remained independent in 1914 - a statement about recognised sovereignty that the gap does not weaken at all. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is that the partition was complete as a legal fact long before it was complete as an administrative one, and that the interval between the two is where most of the violence of the colonial period occurred.

**Residual:** What cannot be settled here is the chronology of effective administration. It holds no data on when particular districts came under working colonial government, no administrative establishment figures, no revenue series and no campaign records. The gap between claim and control is therefore asserted as a structural feature with named illustrations, and its extent in any particular territory is recorded as beyond this material.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the middle of any partition answer, and it is where a script can distinguish itself by describing a mechanism rather than listing colonies. Use the five stages as the organising spine: paper claim, diplomatic claim, occupation, administration, boundary commission. Attach one named instrument to each - the standardised treaty form, the bilateral convention, the chartered company and the column, the district officer and the cash tax, the survey party. Then deploy the partition table accurately, because the examiner will check it, and add one analytical observation about the shape of each empire rather than merely listing holdings. Close with the gap between the map and the ground, because it pre-empts the over-reading of the 1914 figure and sets up the resistance lessons that follow. In a boundary question, the straight-line mechanism of Section 2 - a line described by coordinates between governments that had visited neither end of it - is worth more than any amount of general comment about arbitrariness.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating the partition as completed in 1885, when conversion ran into the twentieth century.
- Reading the 1914 map as a map of administration rather than of claims.
- Describing treaties as agreements between equals, when capacity, meaning and audience were all defective.
- Presenting chartered companies as commercial enterprises rather than as holders of delegated sovereign power.
- Forgetting that colonial armies were overwhelmingly composed of African soldiers under European officers.
- Explaining boundary arbitrariness by malice rather than by method - lines agreed between governments describing unvisited territory.
- Misattributing holdings in the partition table, which is an easily checked factual error.
- Narrating the transfer of German colonies as mandates after 1919, which belongs to the First World War topic.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Five stages of conversion: paper claim; diplomatic claim; occupation; administration; boundary commission.
2. ⚠️ The treaty form's three defects: capacity to sign, meaning of what was signed, and a European audience.
3. ✅ Royal Niger Company 1886; Imperial British East Africa Company 1888; British South Africa Company 1889 with Cecil Rhodes; German East Africa Company with Carl Peters.
4. ✅ Chartered or concessionary control mixed private and state interests; profit and sovereignty overlapped; companies generally failed into state administration.
5. ⚠️ Columns were small forces of African soldiers under European officers with modern firearms - the conquest was carried out very largely by Africans in European service.
6. ✅ Boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities; the mechanism is lines agreed in Europe describing unvisited territory.
7. ✅ Partition table: Britain - Egypt, Sudan, South Africa, Nigeria, East African holdings; France - much of West and Equatorial Africa, Algeria, Madagascar; Belgium - Congo; Germany - Togo, Cameroon, German East Africa, German South West Africa; Portugal - Angola, Mozambique; Italy - Eritrea, Somalia, and failure in Ethiopia.
8. ✅ By 1914 only Ethiopia and Liberia remained independent.
9. ⚠️ Shapes: Britain longitudinal and strategic; France a contiguous bloc large in area and small in value; Germany scattered and status-driven; Portugal coastal positions extended inland.
10. ⚠️ The structural generalisation: the poorer the metropolitan power relative to its claim, the more it relied on compulsion.
11. ✅ Portugal's rose-coloured map abandoned after the British ultimatum of 1890.
12. ✅ The Anglo-German agreement of 1890: East African boundaries settled, Heligoland transferred to Germany, Britain's position in Zanzibar recognised - African territory as diplomatic currency.
13. ✅ The recorded flow: Berlin rules and effective occupation, then partition, then extraction, resistance and artificial borders - resistance follows the map.
14. ⚠️ The 1914 map records claims; large areas were not yet administered; the colonial state was thin from the outset.

### Concept check

**Question:** Set out what had to happen between a treaty signed in an African village in 1885 and a functioning colonial district in 1910, and explain why that interval matters for how the 1914 map should be read.

**Model answer:** Five distinct things had to happen, performed by different people over decades. A paper claim had to be obtained, usually on a standardised European-language treaty form explained by an interested interpreter, which might bind nobody because in many societies no individual could alienate collective rights and because a ruler might understand friendship where the text recorded cession. That claim had to be converted into a diplomatic claim by notification to rival powers and by a bilateral convention describing a boundary, frequently by coordinates or rivers across territory neither party had visited. Occupation had to follow, through a chartered company or a column of African soldiers under European officers with modern firearms, and the company often failed and passed its territory to the state. Administration had to be established - taxation in cash or labour, recognised or appointed chiefs, a district officer, courts and a railway to the coast - and it was this stage, not conquest, that provoked the Maji Maji uprising of 1905-07 against labour and taxation demands. Finally a boundary commission had to survey and mark the line, with the recorded result that boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities. The interval matters because the 1914 map records the second stage and not the fourth: it shows claims recognised among Europeans, not districts under working government, so the figure of about 90 per cent of African territory under European control describes recognised claims rather than administered territory, and the recorded sequence runs from Berlin's rules through partition to extraction, resistance and artificial borders - with much of the fighting following the map rather than preceding it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that a thin colonial presence means a light one. A thin administration holding a monopoly of modern force and an absolute revenue requirement governs by exemplary punishment, collective penalty and compelled labour precisely because it lacks the personnel to govern otherwise, so incompleteness of administration is an explanation of coercion rather than a mitigation of it.

*Conversion had one further consequence that the five stages do not capture. When two powers converting claims in opposite directions arrived at the same place, the procedure that had kept the peace was tested directly. The next lesson takes the four occasions on which imperial competition became a crisis - and marks precisely where this topic hands over to the topic on 1914.*

## Lesson 13 - Four flashpoints: Adwa, Fashoda, South Africa and Morocco

Progress: 13/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Adwa 1896 as a defeat of a European power; Fashoda 1898 and the crossing of two axes; the South African War of 1899-1902 and the settler-mineral colony; the Moroccan crises of 1905-06 and 1911 as imperial competition displaced into Europe; what the four cases jointly establish; the exact boundary with the 1914 topic

Book context: queried - the standard account's timeline entry for 1896, Ethiopia defeats Italy at Adwa, with its significance that African resistance could succeed, and its partition-table entry recording Italian failure in Ethiopia; the core world-history source's record that Abyssinia was the only remaining independent state left in Africa in 1935 and that Italian involvement went back to 1896, when an Italian attempt to colonise it ended in defeat at Adowa; its account of the Boer War of 1899-1902, the British defeat of the Transvaal and the Orange Free State and the formation of the Union of South Africa in 1910, with the racial composition and political exclusion of the new state, and its verdict-section record of concentration camps during that war; its account of the Moroccan Crisis of 1905-06, the Algeciras conference of January 1906, the Entente Cordiale of 1904 and Morocco as one of the few remaining areas of Africa not controlled by a European power; and its account of the Agadir Crisis of 1911, the occupation of Fez, the gunboat Panther, and the German recognition of the French protectorate in return for two strips of the French Congo; its map of Africa marking Fashoda

### Visual: four crises, four different kinds of failure

```text
   CRISIS              WHO COLLIDED              WHAT IT PROVED
   =========================================================================
   ADWA                an EUROPEAN power and     that the partition was
   1896                an AFRICAN state          CONTINGENT, not inevitable
                                                 -> Ethiopia remains
                                                    independent to 1914
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   FASHODA             two EUROPEAN powers       that the procedural peace
   1898                on the SAME RIVER         HELD, but only just
                       Britain's north-south     -> resolved by withdrawal,
                       axis crosses France's        not by war
                       west-east axis            -> followed by the
                                                    Entente Cordiale, 1904
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   SOUTH AFRICA        an IMPERIAL power and     that a SETTLER-MINERAL
   1899-1902           SETTLER REPUBLICS of      colony generates its own
                       European origin           imperial wars, and that
                                                 the settlement excluded
                                                 the African majority
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   MOROCCO             two EUROPEAN powers,      that by 1905 African
   1905-06 and 1911    with the dispute          disputes had become
                       CARRIED BACK INTO         EUROPEAN alignment tests
                       EUROPEAN DIPLOMACY        -> HANDED TO THE 1914 OWNER
   =========================================================================
   THE PATTERN ACROSS THE FOUR:
   1896  Africa can still defeat Europe
   1898  Europe can still avoid fighting Europe over Africa
   1902  the costliest imperial war is fought against Europeans in Africa
   1911  Africa is no longer the subject of the quarrel; it is the counter
```

*Read the last block downward. In fifteen years the character of imperial competition changes completely: an African state defeats a European army; two European powers nearly fight and then settle; a European power fights other Europeans in Africa at great cost; and finally an African territory becomes merely the token in a European alignment crisis. That trajectory is the bridge from this topic to the topic on the world in 1914, and knowing exactly where it ends is what keeps an answer inside its boundary.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The procedure agreed at Berlin worked. Europeans partitioned a continent without going to war with each other, which was not a foregone conclusion and which was, from their point of view, the procedure's principal achievement.

But procedures work by keeping disputes away from the point at which force becomes the only remaining option, and on four occasions the point was approached or passed. Each of the four failed differently, and each teaches something different.

At Adwa, the failure was of the assumption underlying the whole enterprise: that European forces would win. At Fashoda, the failure was of the assumption that spheres agreed on paper would not collide on the ground. In South Africa, the failure was of the assumption that imperial expansion would be cheap. And in Morocco, the character of the competition changed altogether: what was formally a dispute about an African territory became a test of European alliances, at which point the story ceases to be about Africa at all and passes to another topic.

### 2. Adwa, 1896: the defeat of a European power

✅ **Fact:** the standard account records **1896 - Ethiopia defeats Italy at Adwa**, with the significance: **African resistance could succeed**.

✅ **Fact:** its partition table records Italy's holdings as **Eritrea, Somalia**, and records that Italy **failed in Ethiopia at Adwa**.

✅ **Fact:** it records Adwa as the case of **successful armed resistance**, proving that **conquest was contingent, not inevitable**, with the caution that **Adwa is exceptional** and that **most resistance was defeated**.

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source records, in its account of the Italian invasion of 1935, that Ethiopia was **the only remaining independent state left in Africa**, and that **Italian involvement in the country went back to 1896, when an Italian attempt to colonise it had ended in ignominious defeat at Adowa**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard particulars are that the battle was fought on **1 March 1896** near Adwa in northern Ethiopia; that the Ethiopian forces were commanded by the Emperor **Menelik II**; that the immediate origin of the war was a dispute over the interpretation of the **Treaty of Wichale** of **1889**, whose Italian text was read as establishing an Italian protectorate while the Amharic text was not; and that the war was closed by a treaty in the same year, **1896**, in which Italy recognised Ethiopian independence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** why Ethiopia and nowhere else? Four conditions converged, and stating them is what turns Adwa from an anecdote into an argument.

| Condition | Why it mattered |
|---|---|
| **A unified state with a single command** | ⚠️ Menelik could mobilise and direct a large army as one force, which decentralised societies and rival kingdoms elsewhere could not |
| **Modern firearms in quantity** | ⚠️ The firepower gap of Lesson 7 was the decisive European advantage, and Ethiopia had substantially closed it by purchasing modern weapons - which is why European restriction of arms sales to African states mattered so much |
| **Terrain and interior lines** | ⚠️ A highland campaign far from the coast strained Italian supply while Ethiopian forces fought close to their own bases |
| **A weak and divided adversary** | ⚠️ Italy was the least resourced of the colonising powers, and its expedition was under-supplied for the scale of the task |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the caution attached by the standard account is as important as the fact. Adwa proves that conquest was contingent; it does not prove that it was generally resistible. The four conditions above were rarely available together, and an answer that treats Adwa as representative of African military capacity has overstated exactly what the record warns against.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two further consequences belong in a strong answer. First, Adwa preserved the exception that defines the map of 1914: ✅ only Ethiopia and Liberia remained independent. Second, the defeat was remembered as a humiliation and became a stated motive for the invasion of 1935 - ✅ the core source records the 1935 attack as, among other things, intended to avenge the defeat of 1896. ⚠️ That later invasion belongs to the fascism and inter-war topics and is named here in one clause, not narrated.

### 3. Fashoda, 1898: two axes cross

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source's map of Africa in the period marks **Fashoda** in the Anglo-Egyptian Sudan, on the upper Nile.

✅ **Fact:** the standard particulars are that a small French expedition under **Jean-Baptiste Marchand**, having crossed Africa from the west, occupied **Fashoda** on the Upper Nile in **July 1898**; that a much larger Anglo-Egyptian force under **Herbert Kitchener**, having destroyed the Mahdist state's army at **Omdurman** on **2 September 1898**, moved south and confronted it; that the two governments came close to war; and that France withdrew in **November 1898**, the dispute being settled by an Anglo-French declaration in **1899** that fixed their respective spheres.

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source records the sequel: the **Entente Cordiale of 1904**, containing the understanding that **France would recognise Britain's position in Egypt in return for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical value of Fashoda is geometric, and it was set up in Lesson 5. A British strategic axis running longitudinally from Egypt down the Nile and through East Africa had to cross a French axis running latitudinally from the Atlantic coast eastward across the Sahel. Two perpendicular lines meet at exactly one point, and the upper Nile is where they met. ⚠️ An answer that states the geometry rather than narrating the episode is doing analysis.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three conclusions follow.

1. **The procedural peace held, but at its limit.** The crisis was resolved by withdrawal and a boundary declaration - which is the Berlin mechanism working - but only after both governments had contemplated war.
2. **The resolution produced an alignment.** ✅ The Entente Cordiale of 1904 settled the Egyptian and Moroccan questions together, converting the two powers' principal colonial quarrel into a colonial bargain.
3. **The bargain created the next crisis.** ⚠️ It is precisely the Moroccan half of the 1904 understanding that Germany challenged in 1905, which is the connection to Section 5.

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** ✅ the Anglo-Egyptian campaign that preceded Fashoda destroyed the army of the Mahdist state at Omdurman in a battle in which the disparity in firepower was extreme. ⚠️ **No casualty figures are given here for Omdurman or for any other engagement in this session**, because none is held here. The Mahdist state itself is treated in Lesson 14 as a case of African resistance, which is where it belongs.

### 4. South Africa, 1899-1902: the settler-mineral colony and its war

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source records the sequence: the **Cape captured by Britain in 1795**; confirmed British by the **1814** settlement; British settlers arriving in Cape Colony; Dutch settlers becoming restless, **especially when the British government made all slaves free throughout the British Empire in 1838**; the **Great Trek (1835-40)** northwards, with some settlement in **Natal**, and the **later independent republics recognised 1852 and 1854** - the **Transvaal** by the **Sand River Convention of 1852** and the **Orange Free State** by the **Bloemfontein Convention of 1854**; and, in the **Boer War of 1899-1902**, the British defeat of the Transvaal and the Orange Free State, followed in **1910** by their union with Cape Colony and Natal to form the **Union of South Africa**.

✅ **Fact:** the same source records the composition and political character of the new state: approximately **70 per cent** black Africans, **18 per cent** whites of European origin (of whom about **60 per cent** were Dutch and the remainder British), **9 per cent** people of mixed descent, and **3 per cent** Asians; that **whites dominated politics and the economic life of the new state** and that, with few exceptions, **black people were not allowed to vote**; and that black people **had to do most of the manual work in factories, in the gold mines and on farms**, with men **mostly living in barracks accommodation away from their wives and children**.

✅ **Fact:** in its verdict on the imperial record, the same source records that **during the Boer War (1899-1902) in South Africa, the British set up concentration camps in which about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died**.

✅ **Fact:** the standard particulars connecting these are that major mineral discoveries in the southern African interior in the later nineteenth century transformed the region's economic and strategic value; that **Cecil Rhodes**, who combined mining wealth with the premiership of Cape Colony and control of the **British South Africa Company** chartered in **1889**, pursued northward expansion; and that the failed **Jameson Raid** into the Transvaal in **1895-96** intensified the confrontation that preceded the war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four points make this case examinable rather than merely narrative.

1. **It is the only war of the partition fought principally between Europeans in Africa**, and it was the most expensive imperial war of the period - which is decisive evidence against any assumption that imperial expansion was cheap.
2. **It is the clearest case of the settler-mineral colony as a distinct type**, in which a resident European population with its own political institutions generates conflicts that the metropole must then resolve.
3. **The settlement excluded the African majority entirely.** ✅ The Union of 1910 produced a state in which approximately 70 per cent of the population was black African and in which, with few exceptions, black people were not allowed to vote, while performing most of the manual labour in factories, mines and farms and living, in the case of male migrant workers, in barracks away from their families. ⚠️ The war between two European communities was therefore concluded by a compromise at the expense of a third party who had not been consulted - which is the South African version of the Berlin Conference's defining absence.
4. **The migrant-labour compound system recorded here is the mechanism of Lesson 17** in its most developed form, and it should be carried forward rather than left in the war narrative.

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline on the camps:** the camp figure above is carried **exactly as the core source states it**, attributed to that source, as a proportion of the Boer population. ⚠️ No separate figure is held here for the camps in which black African civilians were held during the same war, and none is supplied or estimated here; the absence is recorded rather than filled. ⚠️ The conduct is attributed to the military policy of the British command in that campaign, not to a people.

### 5. Morocco, 1905-06 and 1911: the quarrel leaves Africa

✅ **Fact:** the core world-history source records the **Moroccan Crisis of 1905-06** as an attempt by Germany **to expand their empire and to test the recently signed Anglo-French Entente Cordiale of 1904**, under which **France would recognise Britain's position in Egypt in return for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco**; it records Morocco as **one of the few remaining areas of Africa not controlled by a European power**; that Germany announced it would assist the Sultan to maintain the country's independence and demanded an international conference; that the conference was held at **Algeciras in southern Spain in January 1906**; that Britain led the opposition to Germany; that **Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain supported the French demand to control the Moroccan bank and police**; and that the outcome was **a serious diplomatic defeat for the Germans**, followed by Anglo-French military conversations.

✅ **Fact:** it records the **Agadir Crisis of 1911**: **French troops occupied Fez**, the Moroccan capital, to put down a rebellion against the Sultan, and it appeared that France was about to annex Morocco; **Germany sent a gunboat, the Panther, to the Moroccan port of Agadir**, hoping to pressure France into compensation, **perhaps the French Congo**; Britain was concerned that Germany might acquire Agadir as a naval base threatening British trade routes; **Lloyd George** used a speech at the Mansion House to warn Germany that Britain would not stand by **where her interests were vitally affected**; **France stood firm**, the gunboat was withdrawn, and **Germany agreed to recognise the French protectorate over Morocco in return for two strips of territory in the French Congo**; the outcome was seen as a triumph for the Entente powers, and German public opinion became intensely anti-British.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two crises are included in this session for one reason and must be used for that reason only. They demonstrate the **terminal stage** of imperial competition in Africa, in which the African territory is no longer the object of the dispute but its counter. ⚠️ In 1911 the substantive transaction is an exchange of a Moroccan protectorate for two strips of Central African territory, negotiated between two European powers in order to settle a question about European alignments. The African populations concerned are, in the record as it stands, transferred.

⚠️ **Boundary, stated exactly:** the Entente Cordiale, the Anglo-Russian agreement, the alliance systems, the naval race, the Balkan crises and the outbreak of war in 1914 belong to the **topic on the world in 1914**. This session records the two Moroccan crises as the point at which African questions became European alignment tests, and **stops there**. Nothing further about the road to war is narrated here.

### 6. What the four cases jointly establish

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four conclusions, each usable as a single sentence.

1. **Conquest was contingent.** ✅ Adwa proves that European victory was not automatic, while the caution that Adwa was exceptional prevents the conclusion from being over-extended.
2. **The procedural peace among Europeans was real but strained.** Fashoda was resolved without war; Morocco produced two international crises; and the resolution mechanism in both cases was compensation elsewhere in Africa.
3. **Imperial expansion was not cheap.** The South African War is the counter-example that should be deployed whenever an answer claims that colonies were acquired at negligible cost.
4. **Africans were absent from every settlement.** ✅ At Berlin there was no African representation; at Algeciras the subject of the conference was the future of a country whose ruler's independence was the stated pretext; in 1911 African territory was exchanged between two European powers; and in 1910 a South African settlement was reached between two European communities over a population that was approximately 70 per cent black African and almost entirely disenfranchised. ⚠️ That consistency is not incidental; it is the structural signature of the whole system.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** Including the Moroccan crises in a lesson about the Scramble for Africa is a category error. By 1905 these are episodes in European diplomacy, whose real subject is the Entente, the alliance blocs and the approach of a European war; treating them as African flashpoints inflates this topic at the expense of the topic that actually holds them.

**Reply:** The objection is right about where the crises lead and wrong about what they show, and the lesson is constructed to respect both halves. Three points. First, the crises are used here for a claim about *imperialism*, not about the alliance system: ✅ the record is that Morocco was one of the few remaining areas of Africa not controlled by a European power, that the 1905-06 crisis was an attempt by Germany to expand its empire and to test the Entente, and that the 1911 settlement transferred a Moroccan protectorate in exchange for two strips of the French Congo. Those facts belong to the history of the partition, because they record how the last unclaimed area of Africa was finally allocated. Second, the transition the crises demonstrate is itself an examinable finding about this topic: the partition ends when African territory ceases to be the object of competition and becomes its currency, and no other episode shows that as clearly. Third, the boundary is observed in practice and not merely asserted: this session narrates the two crises and their African outcomes and stops, handing the Entente, the Anglo-Russian agreement, the naval race, the Balkan crises and the July decisions to the topic on the world in 1914. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is that Morocco belongs to this topic as an ending and to the 1914 topic as a beginning, and the two uses are compatible provided neither annexes the other.

**Residual:** What cannot be settled here is the internal decision-making in any of the four crises. It holds no diplomatic documents, no cabinet records, no campaign papers and no casualty data, so this session records the sequence of events, the outcomes and the recorded contemporary assessments, and does not attribute intentions beyond what the sources state. The magnitudes of the Ethiopian and Anglo-Egyptian campaigns, and the number of Africans held in camps in South Africa, are recorded as unavailable here.

### UPSC application

The four cases supply the evidence for several different demands. On African agency, Adwa is the decisive case, with the four conditions that made it possible and the caution that it was exceptional. On imperial rivalry, Fashoda is the best worked example, with the perpendicular-axes geometry and the observation that the crisis was resolved by withdrawal and compensation. On cost, the South African War is the counter-example to any claim that empire was acquired cheaply, and the Union of 1910 supplies the sharpest single illustration that colonial settlements were concluded over the heads of African majorities. On the transition to the pre-1914 crisis system, the Moroccan crises supply the hand-over sentence and nothing more. Two disciplines are examined with this material: give no casualty figures except the one the core source states for the camps, attributed to that source, and stop at the boundary rather than drifting into the alliance system.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Treating Adwa as evidence that African resistance was generally capable of defeating European armies, against the recorded caution that it was exceptional.
- Omitting the four conditions that made Adwa possible, which converts an argument into an anecdote.
- Narrating the Italian invasion of 1935, which belongs to the inter-war topics.
- Describing Fashoda without the geometry, which is the only part of it that is analytically load-bearing.
- Supplying casualty figures for Omdurman, Adwa or the South African War, none of which is held here.
- Reporting the camp figure without attributing it to the source that states it, or extending it to populations for which no figure is held.
- Treating the South African War as a colonial war against Africans, when it was fought principally between Europeans - while omitting that its settlement excluded the African majority.
- Drifting from the Moroccan crises into the alliance system, the naval race or the July crisis, which belong to the 1914 topic.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1896: Ethiopia defeats Italy at Adwa; African resistance could succeed; conquest was contingent, not inevitable; but Adwa was exceptional and most resistance was defeated.
2. ✅ Adwa fought 1 March 1896; Ethiopian forces under Menelik II; origin in the disputed Treaty of Wichale of 1889; closed by a treaty of 1896 recognising Ethiopian independence.
3. ⚠️ Four conditions for Adwa: unified command; modern firearms in quantity; favourable terrain and interior lines; a weak and under-supplied adversary.
4. ✅ Ethiopia was the only remaining independent African state in 1935, and the 1935 invasion was intended in part to avenge 1896 - named, not narrated.
5. ✅ Fashoda: Marchand's expedition occupied Fashoda on the Upper Nile in July 1898; Kitchener's Anglo-Egyptian force arrived after Omdurman on 2 September 1898; France withdrew in November 1898; spheres fixed by an Anglo-French declaration in 1899.
6. ⚠️ The geometry: a British longitudinal axis crossing a French latitudinal axis meets at one point - the upper Nile.
7. ✅ Entente Cordiale 1904: France recognises Britain's position in Egypt; Britain approves a possible French takeover of Morocco.
8. ✅ South Africa: Cape 1795 and 1814; emancipation throughout the British Empire in 1838 and Boer discontent; Great Trek (1835-40); later independent republics recognised 1852 and 1854 - the Transvaal by the Sand River Convention of 1852 and the Orange Free State by the Bloemfontein Convention of 1854; Natal; Boer War 1899-1902; Union of South Africa 1910.
9. ✅ Union composition and exclusion: about 70 per cent black African, 18 per cent white (about 60 per cent of them Dutch), 9 per cent mixed descent, 3 per cent Asian; whites dominated politics and the economy; black people with few exceptions could not vote; most manual work in factories, gold mines and farms; male migrant workers in barracks away from families.
10. ✅ The core source records that during the Boer War the British set up concentration camps in which about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died - attributed to that source; no figure is held here for African camps.
11. ✅ Mineral discoveries transformed the southern interior; Cecil Rhodes and the British South Africa Company chartered in 1889; the failed Jameson Raid of 1895-96.
12. ✅ Moroccan Crisis 1905-06: a German attempt to expand empire and test the Entente; Morocco one of the few remaining areas of Africa not under European control; Algeciras conference January 1906; Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain supported France; a serious diplomatic defeat for Germany.
13. ✅ Agadir Crisis 1911: French occupation of Fez; the gunboat Panther at Agadir; Lloyd George's Mansion House warning; France stood firm; Germany recognised the French protectorate in return for two strips of the French Congo.
14. ⚠️ The four cases jointly: conquest was contingent; the European peace held but strained; expansion was not cheap; Africans were absent from every settlement.
15. ⚠️ Boundary: the alliance system, the naval race and the July crisis belong to the 1914 topic and are not narrated here.

### Concept check

**Question:** Trace the change in the character of imperial competition in Africa across the four crises between 1896 and 1911, and state precisely where this topic hands over to another topic.

**Model answer:** In 1896 the competition was still between Europe and Africa, and Africa could win: Ethiopia defeated Italy at Adwa on 1 March 1896 under Menelik II, in a war arising from the disputed Treaty of Wichale of 1889, and the outcome preserved one of the two states that were still independent in 1914 - proof that conquest was contingent rather than inevitable, though the record cautions that Adwa was exceptional and most resistance was defeated. In 1898 the competition was between two European powers over African ground: Marchand's expedition at Fashoda and Kitchener's force after Omdurman brought a British longitudinal axis and a French latitudinal axis to the single point at which they had to cross, and the crisis was settled by French withdrawal and a boundary declaration, followed by the Entente Cordiale of 1904 in which France recognised Britain's position in Egypt in return for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco. Between 1899 and 1902 the competition was between Europeans resident in Africa and their metropole, in the costliest imperial war of the period, concluded by the Union of South Africa in 1910 - a settlement between two European communities over a population approximately 70 per cent black African and almost entirely disenfranchised. By 1905-06 and 1911 the African territory had become the counter rather than the subject: Germany's challenge over Morocco was an attempt to expand its empire and to test the Entente, it was defeated diplomatically at Algeciras in January 1906, and in 1911 the Agadir crisis ended with German recognition of the French protectorate in exchange for two strips of the French Congo. The hand-over point is exactly there: the alliance system, the Anglo-Russian agreement, the naval race, the Balkan crises and the outbreak of war in 1914 belong to the topic on the world in 1914, and this topic stops at the African outcomes of the two Moroccan crises.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the Moroccan crises to narrate the road to the First World War. Their function in this topic is to show the terminal stage of the partition, in which the last unclaimed African territory was allocated through a European bargain; carrying the narrative further trespasses on another topic and loses time that the African content of the question requires.

*The European side of the four crises is complete. But two of them - Adwa and the destruction of the Mahdist army that preceded Fashoda - were African military events, and they belong to a question the session has so far deferred. Why did African armies lose? And why, on one occasion, did one of them win? The next lesson answers both.*

## Lesson 14 - African armed resistance: the cases, the pattern, and the one exception

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: resistance at conquest and revolt under rule distinguished; Ethiopia, the Zulu kingdom, Samori Ture, the Mahdist state; Maji Maji 1905-07; the Herero and Nama genocide 1904-08; the five reasons resistance generally failed; why Adwa succeeded; the evidentiary limits on writing about defeat

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the standard account's resistance table recording Zulu, Samori Ture and Maji Maji resistance and requiring that African agency be recognised; its agency evidence bank distinguishing successful armed resistance at Adwa, sustained armed resistance by the Zulu and Samori Ture, mass anti-colonial revolt under rule in Maji Maji of 1905-07 against labour and taxation demands, and the extremity of colonial response in the Herero and Nama genocide of 1904-08, with explicit instructions to give no casualty figures and to name the genocide precisely; the historiographical companion's case table and its finding that resistance was uneven because colonial powers had advantages in logistics, weapons and divided local contexts, and its instruction to write that Europe imposed while African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two different things called resistance

```text
   TYPE A: RESISTANCE AT CONQUEST                 TYPE B: REVOLT UNDER RULE
   defending sovereignty against an               rising against what colonial
   invading force                                 administration DOES
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   the opponent is an invading column             the opponent is a tax
   the aim is to prevent annexation               collector, a labour recruiter,
   defeat ends independence                       a concession company
                                                  the aim is to end a burden
   EXAMPLES IN THIS SESSION:                      defeat ends a rebellion, and
   Ethiopia 1896 (SUCCESS)                        usually intensifies control
   the Zulu kingdom
   Samori Ture's empire                           EXAMPLES IN THIS SESSION:
   the Mahdist state in Sudan                     Maji Maji, German East Africa,
                                                  1905-07, against LABOUR AND
                                                  TAXATION demands
                                                  Herero and Nama, German South
                                                  West Africa, 1904-08, met with
                                                  a campaign recorded as the
                                                  GENOCIDE OF THE HERERO AND NAMA
   =========================================================================
   WHY THE DISTINCTION IS WORTH MARKS:
   Type A tells you about African STATES and their military capacity.
   Type B tells you about COLONIAL RULE and what it was doing to people.
   An answer that merges them can use neither properly, and will
   also get the CHRONOLOGY wrong - Type B is LATER than the partition,
   because it is a response to administration, not to invasion.
```

*The distinction also disposes of a common confusion about dates. Maji Maji in 1905-07 and the Herero and Nama war from 1904 occur twenty years after the Berlin Conference, which makes no sense if they are read as resistance to conquest and perfect sense once they are read as responses to what colonial administration was extracting.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The question "why did Africans lose?" is badly formed in two ways, and correcting it is most of the work.

First, it assumes a single contest. There was no single contest. There were dozens of separate wars, fought by different polities, at different times, against different powers, with different weapons and different outcomes - and, in at least one case, with an African victory.

Second, it assumes that losing is the interesting fact. It is not. The interesting facts are the pattern of the losses, which is highly regular and therefore explicable; the identity of the exception, which is what allows the pattern to be tested; and the fact that resistance continued after defeat, in forms that the word "resistance" in its military sense does not capture at all.

So this lesson asks three questions instead of one. Who resisted, and in what form? Why did that resistance generally fail? And what were the conditions under which it succeeded, on the single occasion when it did?

### 2. What the standard accounts record

✅ **Fact:** the standard account records that **Zulu, Samori Ture, Maji Maji and others resisted**, and that **African agency must be recognised**; it records that **resistance was frequent, though often defeated**; and it corrects the claim that Africans did not resist.

✅ **Fact:** the same account distinguishes the forms of agency precisely:
- **successful armed resistance** - ✅ Ethiopia defeated Italy at **Adwa, 1896**, proving conquest was contingent; ⚠️ caution: Adwa is exceptional and most resistance was defeated;
- **sustained armed resistance** - ✅ **Zulu resistance**; ✅ **Samori Ture in West Africa**, showing **organised states and leaders, not "tribes"**; ⚠️ caution: different polities resisted for different reasons;
- **mass anti-colonial revolt under rule** - ✅ the **Maji Maji uprising, German East Africa, 1905-07**, showing that resistance continued **after conquest, against labour and taxation demands**; ⚠️ caution: **do not give casualty figures**;
- **the extremity of colonial response** - ✅ the **Herero and Nama genocide, German South West Africa, 1904-08**, recorded as **the strongest single evidence against the "civilising mission" claim**; ⚠️ caution: **name it precisely as genocide of the Herero and Nama; do not generalise the term to all colonial violence**.

✅ **Fact:** the historiographical companion records the general finding: the strongest corrective to Eurocentric writing is that **African societies were not passive recipients of conquest**, but **resistance was uneven because colonial powers had advantages in logistics, weapons and divided local contexts**.

✅ **Fact:** it supplies the formula to use and the formula to avoid: ✅ **do not write "Europe acted -> Africa received"**; ✅ **write instead "Europe imposed + African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered"**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that four-verb formula is the structural instruction for both this lesson and the next. This lesson covers the first verb and part of the fourth; Lesson 15 covers the second and third. Splitting them across two lessons is deliberate, because merging them is what produces the standard one-paragraph treatment in which resistance is mentioned and agency is asserted without being demonstrated.

### 3. The four cases of armed defence, and what each shows

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** each case should carry one analytical point rather than a narrative.

| Case | What is recorded | The analytical point it carries |
|---|---|---|
| **Ethiopia** | ✅ Defeated Italy at **Adwa, 1896**; ✅ remained independent in 1914; ✅ recorded by the core source as the only remaining independent African state in 1935 | ⚠️ Proves that the outcome of conquest was contingent on specific conditions rather than determined by a general European superiority. The conditions are in Section 5 |
| **The Zulu kingdom** | ✅ Recorded as sustained armed resistance; ✅ the **Anglo-Zulu War of 1879**, in which a British invasion suffered a severe early defeat before the kingdom was subsequently overcome | ⚠️ Shows that a centralised African state with a disciplined military system could inflict serious defeats, and that European victory frequently required a second and larger effort. It also predates the scramble proper, which is a useful chronological corrective |
| **Samori Ture** | ✅ Recorded as sustained armed resistance in West Africa; ✅ leader of the **Wassoulou empire**, who fought French expansion over roughly two decades from the 1880s, using firearm acquisition, local manufacture and repair, and strategic withdrawal, and was captured in **1898** | ⚠️ The clearest case of an African state adapting militarily and organisationally to the European challenge, which is why the record insists on organised states and leaders rather than "tribes" |
| **The Mahdist state in Sudan** | ✅ A state established after a rising against Egyptian rule, which governed Sudan from the mid-1880s until its army was destroyed at **Omdurman** in **September 1898** and the territory was brought under Anglo-Egyptian control | ⚠️ Shows an African state that defeated one imperial system and was destroyed by another, and connects directly to the Fashoda geometry of Lesson 13 |

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline:** no casualty figure, army size or battle-by-battle chronology is given for any of these campaigns, because none is held here. What is asserted is the polity, the adversary, the approximate span and the outcome.

### 4. The five reasons armed resistance generally failed

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** ✅ the record supplies the headline - advantages in **logistics, weapons and divided local contexts** - and an answer earns more by decomposing it into mechanisms.

| Reason | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **1. The firepower gap** | ⚠️ Breech-loading and magazine rifles and early machine guns defeat mass close-order tactics. ⚠️ The gap was maintained by policy as well as by production, since European powers restricted arms sales to African states |
| **2. Logistics and reinforcement** | ⚠️ A European power could replace a destroyed force; an African state that lost its army generally could not. ⚠️ Steam transport and, later, railways gave the invader operational reach that defenders could not match |
| **3. Divided local contexts** | ✅ Recorded explicitly. ⚠️ Conquest was frequently carried out with African allies who had their own quarrels with the polity being attacked, which converted a European invasion into a local war with European artillery |
| **4. Asymmetry of aims** | ⚠️ A defending state must protect its capital, its population and its economy; an invading column need only win engagements. Defence is therefore structurally more expensive than attack in this contest |
| **5. Sequential rather than simultaneous resistance** | ⚠️ African polities fought at different times, so the same European forces could be used repeatedly against one opponent after another. Simultaneous continental resistance would have been an entirely different contest, and no institution existed that could have organised it |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** reason 5 deserves particular emphasis because it is rarely stated and it is the most structural of the five. The partition was a sequence of local wars, and in a sequence the stronger side's advantages compound. ⚠️ It also disposes of an unfair implicit comparison: African polities are sometimes criticised for failing to unite, but there was no continental political institution through which unity could have been organised, and European states did not unite either - they competed, and their competition was managed only by a recognition rule agreed among themselves.

### 5. Why Adwa succeeded: the exception that tests the rule

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** take the five reasons and invert them, and the Ethiopian case appears as the one in which most of the disadvantages were absent.

| General disadvantage | The Ethiopian exception |
|---|---|
| Firepower gap | ⚠️ Substantially closed by the acquisition of modern firearms before the war |
| Logistics favour the invader | ⚠️ Reversed: a highland campaign far inland strained Italian supply, while Ethiopian forces operated near their own base areas |
| Divided local contexts | ⚠️ Largely absent: a reconstructed central authority under Menelik II could mobilise and direct a unified force |
| Asymmetry of aims | ⚠️ Reduced: the campaign came to a single decisive engagement, which is the one form of contest in which a defender's numerical strength can be brought to bear |
| Sequential resistance | ⚠️ Not applicable: Italy was the least resourced colonising power and was not able to mount a sustained second effort of the kind that overcame other African states |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table is the strongest available demonstration that African defeat was not a natural consequence of anything, and it is the correct use of Adwa. ✅ It is also bounded by the recorded caution that Adwa was exceptional - and an answer should state both, because the value of the case lies exactly in the fact that its conditions were rare.

### 6. Revolt under colonial rule: Maji Maji, and the Herero and Nama

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these are the Type B events of the opening visual, and they must be handled with the evidentiary discipline the record requires.

✅ **Fact:** the **Maji Maji uprising** occurred in **German East Africa** in **1905-07**. ✅ It is recorded as a mass anti-colonial revolt showing that resistance continued **after conquest**, and it was directed **against labour and taxation demands**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points follow. First, its date establishes the chronological argument of Lesson 12: this is resistance to administration, not to invasion, and it occurs two decades after the partition. Second, its causes are the subject of Lesson 17: compulsory cultivation, labour obligations and cash taxation are what a revolt of this kind is against, which makes it the best single piece of evidence about what colonial rule was actually doing. Third, its scale - a movement that drew together communities across a wide area - is itself evidence against the administrative assumption that African societies were fragmented into unconnected units.

✅ **Fact:** the **genocide of the Herero and Nama** took place in **German South West Africa** in **1904-08**. ✅ It is recorded as **the strongest single evidence against the "civilising mission" claim**.

⚠️ **Evidentiary discipline, stated in full because this is where it matters most:**
- ✅ The term is used **precisely**: this is the genocide of the Herero and Nama. ✅ The record's instruction is explicit - **do not generalise the term to all colonial violence**.
- ✅ **No casualty figure is given.** The record's instruction is explicit that none is sourced here, and none is estimated, inferred or implied.
- ⚠️ Attribution is to the **German colonial military command and the policy it executed in that campaign**, not to Germans as a people. The historical claim concerns an identified administration and an identified campaign.
- ⚠️ What may be asserted from the material held here is the **name**, the **place**, the **dates** and the **characterisation** the record supplies, together with the analytical use it identifies - that a campaign of this character stands as the strongest available refutation of the civilising-mission claim.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the analytical relationship between the two events is worth one sentence in an answer. Both occurred in German territories within two years of each other, both arose from land and labour demands on African populations, and both were met with force on a scale far exceeding the threat - which is the pattern Lesson 12 predicted for a thin administration governing by exemplary punishment because it lacked the means to govern by consent.

### Strongest objection, reply and residual

**Objection:** A lesson organised around military resistance still concedes the main point to a Eurocentric framing. It measures African societies by their performance in European-style warfare, treats defeat as the central fact, and reduces a century of African political history to a series of engagements with invaders. Genuine recognition of agency would not be a better account of how Africans lost battles.

**Reply:** The objection is correct about the danger and is met by the structure of the session rather than by a disclaimer. Three points. First, this lesson is explicitly one of two: ✅ the recorded formula is that African societies **resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered**, and Lesson 15 is devoted entirely to negotiation, intermediation, adaptation and state-building - which is the larger part of the record and is where most Africans' experience actually lies. Second, within this lesson the analytical weight is placed on the *conditions* rather than on the outcomes: the five failure mechanisms and the Ethiopian inversion of them are claims about logistics, firearms supply, political unity and sequencing, not about capability or courage, and they are what prevent defeat from being read as a verdict on African societies. Third, the military material is indispensable precisely because it is evidence of political organisation: ✅ the record insists that these were organised states and leaders and not "tribes", and the ability to mobilise, supply, equip and command armies over years is the strongest available demonstration of that claim against the administrative categories examined in Lesson 2. ⚠️ The disciplined formulation is that resistance is taught here as evidence of African political capacity rather than as a scorecard, and that the scorecard reading is exactly what the five-mechanism analysis is designed to prevent.

**Residual:** What cannot be settled here is almost every quantitative and internal question about these conflicts: numbers engaged, casualties, arms holdings, the internal politics of the polities concerned, and the chronology of individual campaigns. It holds no African testimony of any kind. This session therefore teaches the polities, the leaders, the approximate spans, the outcomes and the recorded characterisations, and records the internal history of African resistance as material this base does not contain - an absence which is itself, as Lesson 18 argues, a fact about the colonial record rather than about the past.

### UPSC application

African agency is a recurring demand, and the structure that scores has three parts, of which this lesson supplies the first. Distinguish resistance at conquest from revolt under rule, and use the dates to prove the distinction - Maji Maji in 1905-07 is two decades after the partition and is therefore evidence about colonial administration. Name organised polities and leaders rather than writing generally about African resistance: Ethiopia under Menelik II, the Zulu kingdom, Samori Ture's Wassoulou empire and the Mahdist state, because the record insists on states and leaders rather than "tribes". Then explain the pattern with the five mechanisms and test it with Adwa, stating both that it proves conquest was contingent and that it was exceptional. Two disciplines are examined alongside: name the genocide of the Herero and Nama precisely and give no casualty figure anywhere, and attribute conduct to administrations, commands and policies rather than to peoples.

### Examiner traps in this lesson

- Merging resistance at conquest with revolt under colonial rule, which destroys both the analysis and the chronology.
- Writing generally about "African resistance" without naming polities and leaders, against the record's insistence on organised states.
- Treating Adwa as typical, or treating it as a fluke; it was exceptional and it was explicable, and both must be said.
- Supplying casualty figures for Maji Maji, the Herero and Nama genocide, Omdurman or any other engagement.
- Generalising the term genocide to all colonial violence instead of naming the genocide of the Herero and Nama precisely.
- Attributing atrocity to a nation or a people rather than to an identified command, administration or policy.
- Criticising African polities for failing to unite, when no continental institution existed through which unity could have been organised and European states did not unite either.
- Presenting defeat as evidence about African societies rather than about firearms supply, logistics, local divisions and sequencing.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Resistance was frequent, though often defeated; African agency must be recognised.
2. ⚠️ Two types: resistance at conquest, and revolt under colonial rule. The second is later and is evidence about administration.
3. ✅ Successful armed resistance: Ethiopia at Adwa, 1896 - conquest was contingent, not inevitable; but Adwa was exceptional.
4. ✅ Sustained armed resistance: the Zulu kingdom; Samori Ture in West Africa - organised states and leaders, not "tribes"; different polities resisted for different reasons.
5. ✅ Anglo-Zulu War of 1879 with a severe early British defeat; Samori Ture's Wassoulou empire, roughly two decades of resistance to French expansion from the 1880s, capture in 1898; the Mahdist state governing Sudan from the mid-1880s until Omdurman in September 1898.
6. ✅ Mass revolt under rule: Maji Maji, German East Africa, 1905-07, against labour and taxation demands.
7. ✅ The extremity of colonial response: the genocide of the Herero and Nama, German South West Africa, 1904-08 - the strongest single evidence against the civilising-mission claim.
8. ⚠️ Evidentiary discipline: name the genocide precisely; do not generalise the term; give no casualty figures; attribute to commands, administrations and policies.
9. ✅ Resistance was uneven because colonial powers had advantages in logistics, weapons and divided local contexts.
10. ⚠️ Five failure mechanisms: the firepower gap maintained partly by arms-sale restriction; logistics and replaceable forces; divided local contexts and African allies; asymmetry of aims; sequential rather than simultaneous resistance.
11. ⚠️ Adwa inverts four of the five: firearms acquired, logistics reversed, unified command under Menelik II, a single decisive engagement, and an adversary unable to mount a sustained second effort.
12. ✅ The formula: not "Europe acted, Africa received" but "Europe imposed, and African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered".
13. ⚠️ Maji Maji's scale is evidence against the administrative assumption that African societies were fragmented into unconnected units.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why African armed resistance usually failed, in terms that do not appeal to any general European superiority, and then explain what the Ethiopian victory of 1896 demonstrates about that explanation.

**Model answer:** Five specific mechanisms account for the pattern. The firepower gap was decisive in open battle, and it was maintained not only by European production but by European policy, since arms sales to African states were restricted. Logistics and reinforcement favoured the invader, because a European power could replace a destroyed force while an African state that lost its army generally could not, and steam transport and railways extended the invader's operational reach. Local divisions were exploited systematically, and conquest was frequently carried out with African allies who had their own quarrels with the polity under attack - the record notes explicitly that resistance was uneven because colonial powers had advantages in logistics, weapons and divided local contexts. The aims were asymmetric, since a defending state had to protect its capital, population and economy while an invading column needed only to win engagements. And resistance was sequential rather than simultaneous, so that the same forces could be used against one polity after another, which compounds the stronger side's advantages. The Ethiopian victory at Adwa on 1 March 1896 demonstrates that the explanation is correct, because it is the case in which the mechanisms were inverted: modern firearms had been acquired in quantity, a highland campaign far inland strained Italian supply while Ethiopian forces fought near their bases, a reconstructed central authority under Menelik II could direct a unified army, the campaign came to a single decisive engagement, and Italy was the least resourced colonising power and could not mount a sustained second effort. The record's caution completes the point: Adwa proves conquest was contingent rather than inevitable, while remaining exceptional, because those conditions rarely converged.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining defeat by African disunity as though unity had been an available option that was declined. No continental institution existed through which simultaneous resistance could have been organised, and the European powers did not unite either - they competed, and managed their competition by a recognition rule agreed among themselves.

*Armed defence is the most visible form of African action and the least representative. For most people in most places, the colonial period was navigated rather than fought - through negotiation, service, schooling, migration and the exploitation of whatever opportunities a new order created. The next lesson takes those forms seriously, because they are where the record is richest and where the later history of the continent was actually made.*

## Lesson 15 - Negotiation, intermediation, adaptation: the other three-quarters of African agency

Progress: 15/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why collaboration is an analytical and not a moral category; the thin colonial state and its dependence; chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors; treaty-seeking and the use of European rivalry; mission schooling and the making of an educated class; adaptation to cash crops, migration and new legal forums; the continuity into later political leadership

### Visual: how a handful of Europeans governed a continent
