## What to rehearse, given a nil return

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with no owned question is prepared differently from one with a bank of past papers, and the difference is a matter of method rather than of effort.

1. **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** The six directive families in Lesson 21 exhaust what can realistically be asked, and each has a structure. Practise producing the structure from the stem in under a minute.
2. **Rehearse the controls, because they are what a first attempt gets wrong.** The fourteen countries and the exact conference dates; the two percentages with attribution; the refusal to say Berlin partitioned Africa; the absence of casualty figures; the precise naming of the genocide of the Herero and Nama; the refusal of any OAU document claim.
3. **Rehearse the consequences half separately.** ✅ The syllabus clause names colonisation **and its effect on society**, and the second half - land, labour, households, authority, identity, borders - is the part most likely to be thin under time pressure.
4. **Rehearse the agency demand as four parts, not one.** ✅ The demand map records it as **resistance and collaboration, adaptation and survival**, and a script that answers only the first has answered a quarter of the question.
5. **Rehearse the five hand-offs.** One clause each. A script that trespasses into the 1914 topic's or the decolonisation topic's material loses time and gains nothing.
6. **Use the standard accounts' own probable framings as the practice bank.** ✅ The standard account lists three - explain the causes and mechanisms of the scramble; how far was the Berlin Conference responsible for the partition; analyse the economic and social consequences of New Imperialism in Africa - and ✅ the historiographical companion lists three more - that the Berlin Conference gave legality to imperial greed; whether New Imperialism was driven more by capitalism than by nationalism; and the long-term effects of the scramble on African society and politics. The six original questions below are built to cover all six framings.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - Foundations: the category, the continent and the ladder, Lessons 1 to 3

### Cumulative check A1 - what was new, and what was already there

**Question:** Using the material of the first two lessons, state in what senses the New Imperialism was new, and show how the Africa that existed before 1880 determined the form the partition took.

**Model answer:** It was new in four senses and continuous in a fifth. It was new in form, because control moved from trade and coastal footholds to direct political rule over inland territory, European control having been limited mainly to coasts in the 1870s. It was new in pace, since about 10 per cent of African territory was under European control in the 1870s and about 90 per cent by 1914, with only Ethiopia and Liberia independent at the end. It was new in the field of competitors, which now included recently unified Germany and Italy, whose claims Bismarck backed in 1884. And it was new in its rules, because the Berlin General Act recognised a framework for effective occupation. It was continuous in motive, since European demand for African commodities and routes was centuries old. What Africa already was then determined the method. Against centralised states with armies - Ethiopia, the Zulu kingdom, Samori Ture's empire, the Mahdist state - conquest required campaigns and frequently a second and larger effort. Against trading states and merchant networks, European firms first displaced the African middleman and took the customs revenue. Against decentralised societies, a treaty could be obtained from somebody with no authority to bind anyone, which is why administration afterwards recognised and sometimes invented chiefs, hardening fluid identities into fixed administrative categories. And against mobile pastoral societies, the boundaries drawn later cut the seasonal movement on which the economy depended. The pattern of conquest, of resistance and of administrative failure therefore begins with African political form rather than with European intention.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the pre-1880 continent as background colour. The four political forms are a causal variable, not scenery: they explain where conquest was cheap, where it needed a war, where a fictitious signature sufficed, and where the resulting border would later cut a working economy in half.

### Cumulative check A2 - the ladder, and what the scramble actually requires you to explain

**Question:** If informal empire was cheaper, preferred and already widespread, what exactly does the scramble require an explanation of - and what four conditions supply it?

**Model answer:** It requires an explanation not of European appetite, which was long-standing and roughly constant, but of why the cheap methods of satisfying that appetite stopped being safe. Empire runs along a ladder: trade under African terms, informal empire through commercial treaties, loans, debt supervision and a compliant ruler, spheres of influence binding only European signatories, chartered companies holding delegated sovereign powers, protectorates, and finally colonies. Costs rise up the ladder and only the upper rungs exclude a rival, so governments preferred the lowest rung that worked, and the record confirms the logic, since not all colonies were profitable, rule could be costly and profits were concentrated. Four conditions then made the low rungs unsafe. A rival's exclusive claim destroys influence, because a flag excludes and an understanding does not. The local partner can collapse, as in Egypt in 1882, leaving no one through whom to exercise control and a choice between withdrawal and occupation. The frontier generates its own commitments, as traders, missionaries, companies and officers create situations the metropole must honour or publicly repudiate. And once the Berlin General Act recognised a framework for effective occupation, an unoccupied sphere became an invitation rather than a holding. The scarce good was therefore exclusivity rather than land, and territory was the only form in which exclusivity could be held - which is why powers annexed ground they neither wanted nor could profitably use.

**Misconception to avoid:** Accepting the continuity thesis so completely that the measured change from about 10 to about 90 per cent of African territory is left unexplained. Continuity of motive is compatible with a transformation of method, and the transformation is what has to be explained.

## Block B - Causes: five drivers and their ranking, Lessons 4 to 9

### Cumulative check B1 - the five jobs

**Question:** Assign each of the five drivers a job that no other driver can perform, and run the removal test on each.

**Model answer:** Industrial capitalism is the structural driver: it explains why at all and what for, supplying the standing demand for inputs, outlets and secure supply, and determining the extractive content of colonial rule - the record states that industrial powers wanted raw materials and markets, that empire was tied to industrial capitalism and that colonies were reorganised for extraction. Remove it and there is neither demand nor technical capacity. Strategy is the directional driver: it explains which territories were taken and in what order, from the canal of 1869 through the occupation of Egypt in 1882 to the Nile valley and East Africa, and the record confirms that Suez, sea routes and rival empires mattered and that colonies were military-geopolitical assets. Remove it and the acquisition of commercially worthless territory is inexplicable. Prestige and rivalry form the proximate driver: the record states that they explain the timing and speed of the rush after 1884, when Bismarck backed German claims and the scramble became openly competitive, and it is the only driver that compounds, since each claim raises the cost of not claiming. Remove it and the concentration of the partition into two decades is unexplained. Technology and medicine are permissive: steam transport, improved weapons and medicines increased European reach, converting desire into feasibility, and the record's caution is categorical, that technology explains capability and never intent. Remove it and the interior remains unenterable. Ideology is legitimating: the civilising mission and racial-superiority rhetoric justified expansion and, more importantly, supplied domestic legitimation and shaped the form of rule. Remove it and the expenditure is unauthorisable and the institutions built - mission schooling, racially classified law, compelled labour described as training, administration through recognised chiefs - are arbitrary.

**Misconception to avoid:** Ranking the drivers on a single scale of importance. The question is badly formed as posed; each occupies a different causal position, and the defensible ranking changes with what is being explained - occurrence favours economics, timing favours rivalry, territorial selection favours strategy.

### Cumulative check B2 - the economic thesis under pressure

**Question:** State the strongest objection to an economic explanation of the partition, concede what must be conceded, and rebuild the economic case on ground the objection cannot reach.

**Model answer:** The strongest objection is that the aggregate flows do not match. The overwhelming share of European trade and capital export in the period went to other industrial economies, to the Americas and to older-established territories rather than to newly annexed tropical Africa; many colonies were not profitable, rule could be costly and profits were uneven, rhetorical or concentrated in limited hands; and the timing is wrong, since industrial demand for tropical commodities had been rising for decades before annexation began. That must be conceded in terms, because a candidate who denies it is contradicting the record and is easily refuted. The case is then rebuilt on three grounds the objection cannot reach. First, supply security rather than price: annexation converts a commercial relationship vulnerable to a rival's monopoly or tariff into a political guarantee, and insurance is bought against exclusion rather than for a return, which is why unprofitable annexation is evidence for the argument rather than against it. Second, concentrated interests rather than aggregates: mining finance in southern Africa and concession companies in the Congo basin were a small share of national investment and a very large share of the pressure on colonial ministries. Third, and most decisively, economic structure rather than economic motive: whatever brought each territory under control, colonies were then reorganised for extraction, so the economic character of colonialism is far better evidenced than the economic character of the decision to colonise. The surviving verdict is conditional - industrial capitalism created the demand and the capacity and determined what colonial rule was for, while rivalry determined when and how fast the demand was converted into territory.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Hobson and Lenin as one position. Hobson's argument is that imperialism is a remediable policy produced by a distributional defect, with domestic redistribution as its remedy; Lenin's is that it is a necessary stage of capitalist development, terminating in redivision by war - and misreporting the difference is the commonest historiographical error on this topic.

## Block C - The conference, the execution and the collisions, Lessons 10 to 13

### Cumulative check C1 - from a river dispute to a continental rulebook

**Question:** Explain how a dispute in one river basin produced an international conference, what the resulting instrument actually did, and why the conference nevertheless accelerated a partition it did not carry out.

**Model answer:** The Congo basin was the one place where every major European interest was engaged simultaneously and indivisibly. Leopold II, unable to obtain Belgian state support, proceeded personally through an international association presented as exploratory, scientific and anti-slavery, engaging Stanley to establish stations and obtain treaties along the river from about 1879; Brazza obtained rival French treaties on the northern bank; Portugal asserted historic claims at the mouth; Britain required commercial access; and an Anglo-Portuguese treaty of 1884 intended to settle the mouth was abandoned after opposition from other powers and from commercial interests. A river with a single mouth cannot be divided as a coastline can, so a multilateral settlement became necessary, and the conference met at Berlin from 15 November 1884 to 26 February 1885 with representatives of fourteen countries and no African representation. The General Act recognised a framework for effective occupation applying to new coastal acquisitions, with notification to the other signatories; treated the Congo and Niger as free waters; established freedom of trade in a conventional basin; declared against the slave trade; and provided the basis on which the Congo Free State was recognised under Leopold II. It allocated no territory and drew no colonial boundary. It nonetheless accelerated the partition because a recognition rule changes the cost of remaining on a low rung of control: before it, a sphere of influence was a defensible position; after it, an unoccupied sphere was an invitation to any rival willing to occupy. Every power therefore raced to convert influence into occupation, which is why the correct verdict is that Berlin did not partition Africa but standardised the terms on which partition could be recognised, and was decisive as a legitimising rulebook and negligible as a cartographer.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the Congo Free State by Leopold's ambition alone. Ambition explains the attempt; European mutual distrust explains the success, because recognising a claimant too weak to threaten anyone was, for each power, the cheapest way of denying the basin to its strongest rival.

### Cumulative check C2 - claims, control and collisions

**Question:** Distinguish what the map of 1914 records from what it does not, and use the four flashpoints to show how the character of imperial competition changed between 1896 and 1911.

**Model answer:** The 1914 map records recognised claims, not working administration. Conversion from a claim to a district required five stages: a paper treaty, often on a standardised European-language form explained by an interested interpreter and signed by somebody who might have no authority to bind the society; a diplomatic claim notified to rivals and described in a bilateral convention, frequently by coordinates across unvisited territory; occupation by a chartered company or by a column of African soldiers under European officers; administration through cash taxation, recognised or appointed chiefs, courts and a railway to the coast; and finally a boundary commission, with the recorded result that boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities. Because the second stage outran the fourth, large areas shown in colonial colours were not yet administered, and the recorded flow runs from Berlin's rules through partition to extraction, resistance and artificial borders - which is why Maji Maji in 1905-07 is a revolt against administration rather than against invasion. The flashpoints then show the competition changing character. In 1896 Ethiopia defeated Italy at Adwa, proving conquest contingent rather than inevitable. In 1898 two European powers met at Fashoda where a British longitudinal axis crossed a French latitudinal one, and the crisis was settled by French withdrawal and a boundary declaration rather than by war, with the Entente Cordiale of 1904 trading recognition of Britain's position in Egypt for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco. Between 1899 and 1902 the costliest imperial war of the period was fought principally between Europeans in South Africa, concluded by a Union in 1910 that excluded an African majority of about 70 per cent from the vote. And by 1905-06 and 1911 the African territory had become the counter rather than the subject, with Germany defeated diplomatically at Algeciras and then recognising the French protectorate over Morocco in exchange for two strips of the French Congo.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the 90-per-cent figure as a statement about effective government. It measures claims recognised among Europeans; conquest, administration and boundary marking followed, and much of the fighting of the colonial period came after the map rather than before it.

## Block D - African action and the colonial system, Lessons 14 to 18

### Cumulative check D1 - the four verbs

**Question:** The record instructs that one should write not "Europe acted, Africa received" but "Europe imposed, and African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered". Demonstrate each of the four verbs with evidence.

**Model answer:** They resisted, in two distinguishable forms. At conquest, organised states fought: Ethiopia defeated Italy at Adwa in 1896, the Zulu kingdom and Samori Ture's Wassoulou empire conducted sustained campaigns, and the Mahdist state governed Sudan until Omdurman in 1898 - the record insisting that these were organised states and leaders and not "tribes". Under rule, they revolted: Maji Maji in German East Africa in 1905-07 was directed against labour and taxation demands, two decades after the partition. They negotiated: rulers chose among fighting, seeking the least dangerous protector, playing rivals against each other, and accepting subordination in exchange for retained internal authority - and where a polity's cooperation was valuable, the terms obtained were better, which is why colonial rule varied with African bargaining strength. They adapted: communities adjusted to cash crops, migrant labour, mission schooling and new legal forums, using them where possible, though the adaptation was constrained rather than free; and European rule itself depended on African chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors because the administrative presence was thin, which is how tiny European establishments governed vast territories and why some African elites gained. They suffered: colonial rule often meant coercion and atrocity, with the genocide of the Herero and Nama in German South West Africa in 1904-08 standing as the strongest single evidence against the civilising-mission claim, and with conquest, forced labour, movement and the disruption of food systems raising mortality in several regions - a finding that must be kept qualitative, since no figures are sourced.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using "collaborator" as a moral verdict. The record marks collaboration as an analytical category and not a moral judgement; the alternatives available to an intermediary were exclusion, replacement or punishment, and agency here denotes attributable action under coercion rather than freedom.

### Cumulative check D2 - the system and its discipline

**Question:** Set out the colonial economic engine in its causal steps, and state the three rules that govern every sentence you write about the violence it produced.

**Model answer:** The engine begins with a tax payable in cash, assessed per hut or per adult male and collected through chiefs, imposed on households that farmed largely for their own consumption and used little cash. Such a household could obtain cash only by growing an export crop, by sending members to earn wages in mines, plantations, porterage or public works, or by failing to pay and incurring penalties that frequently included labour in lieu of tax; so the tax functioned as a labour-recruitment device without any order to work being issued, which is exactly what an administration too thin to supervise anything required. Land was simultaneously constrained, by alienation to settlers as on the Kenyan highland plateau, by concession grants to companies, and by the declaration of "vacant" land as state property - expropriation through definition. Structural male absence followed, shifting farming and household responsibility onto women while colonial law and mission institutions redefined marriage and property, and in the developed systems male workers lived in barracks away from their families so that the rural household subsidised the wage. The economy was reoriented outward, with transport built producer-to-port, one or two commodities, no processing and no internal market. The three rules governing any account of the resulting violence are: name precisely, so that the genocide of the Herero and Nama is named as such and the term is not generalised to all colonial violence; assert no number that is not sourced, since no casualty figure for Maji Maji, the Herero and Nama genocide or Congo Free State rule is held; and attribute to identified regimes, administrations, commands, companies and policies rather than to nations or peoples.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the evidentiary discipline weakens the indictment. A systemic account - coercion embedded in the revenue mechanism, exemplary punishment substituting for administration, abolition proclaimed while forced labour was imposed - is a heavier charge than a list of incidents, and it cannot be refuted by an unsourced number.

## Block E - Legacy, debate and conversion, Lessons 19 to 21

### Cumulative check E1 - the inheritance, stated without determinism

**Question:** Name the four inheritances of the partition, explain why the borders were preserved, and state the qualification that keeps the argument from becoming deterministic.

**Model answer:** The four inheritances are artificial frontiers that sharpened later interstate and ethnic tensions; extractive economies whose commodity dependence outlasted empire; administratively fixed identities, since administration recognised and sometimes invented chiefs and thereby hardened fluid identities into fixed categories; and a racial order of hierarchy and exclusion, whose counterpart was the violent memory from which anti-colonial nationalism later drew strength. The borders were preserved because every alternative was worse: reopening one legitimises reopening all, and no state's frontiers would survive a general revision, so border stability was a collective-security choice rather than an oversight. The evidence is behavioural and must be stated as such, with no document claimed: the Organization of African Unity, founded in 1963, became the forum in which African states addressed each other's conflicts; in Nigeria's civil war neither the UN, the Commonwealth nor the OAU would support Biafra's secession; Katanga's secession was reversed with UN troops; and where separation occurred it came by agreement, as when Singapore left the Federation of Malaysia in 1965, which shows that unilateral secession was the thing being prevented. The cost was severe, since Nigeria's unity was preserved only through a war in which Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in the fighting. The qualification is the record's own: border inheritance is not a deterministic explanation for every later conflict, and the same caution applies to the economic and social inheritances - an inheritance sets the starting position and the constraints, and the choices made within them remain the actors'.

**Misconception to avoid:** Claiming that the OAU adopted a formal resolution on border inviolability, or citing a text or article for it. The evidence held here is behavioural; state the mechanism and not a document.

### Cumulative check E2 - the whole topic in one verdict

**Question:** In a single connected argument, account for the partition of Africa between the 1870s and 1914, its execution, its content and its legacy - and state what your evidence cannot settle.

**Model answer:** Industrial capitalism created a standing demand for secure raw materials and markets and the technical capacity to obtain them, which is why about 10 per cent of African territory under European control in the 1870s had become about 90 per cent by 1914, with only Ethiopia and Liberia independent. That demand had long been satisfied by cheaper means - trade, treaties, debt supervision and compliant rulers - and the scramble is the record of those means failing: a local partner collapsed in Egypt in 1882, converting a financial entanglement into an occupation and making the Nile valley a strategic requirement; recently unified Germany and Italy entered the competition for rank, and when Bismarck backed German claims in 1884 the competition became openly competitive; and once the Berlin General Act of 15 November 1884 to 26 February 1885, agreed by fourteen states without African representation, recognised a framework for effective occupation, an unoccupied sphere became an invitation. Steam, telegraph, prophylaxis and modern firearms made conquest cheap; the civilising mission and racial science made it authorisable. Execution followed the rules rather than accompanying them, through treaty forms, chartered companies, columns of African soldiers under European officers and boundary commissions whose lines rarely followed ethnic or political realities. African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered, and colonial rule depended on African chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors because the European presence was thin. The content of that rule was extraction: cash taxation compelling export cultivation and labour migration, land alienated or declared vacant, households restructured and women's burdens increased, and coercion that ranged from exemplary punishment to the genocide of the Herero and Nama in 1904-08. The legacy was structural rather than territorial - economies reoriented to export, administrations built for control and identities administratively fixed - and the borders, kept because a rule of revision would have had no stopping point, made those legacies harder to escape. What the evidence cannot settle is every magnitude: no trade, investment, revenue, output, casualty or living-standard figure is held, no African testimony survives in it, and no ranking of causes can be demonstrated for the continent as a whole.

**Misconception to avoid:** Ending the account at the partition. The syllabus clause names colonisation and its effect on society, and a verdict that stops at the map has answered half a clause with all of the available words.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These six questions are original to this session and are not verified PYQs; the topic owns none, and the two standard accounts held for this topic record six probable framings, all of which are covered below. Attempt each under timed conditions before reading the model answer. Each model sits at or below its stated word ceiling - 150 words at 10 marks and 250 words at 15 and 20 marks, the established repository convention - and each carries a scoring note identifying the single element that most often separates a good script from an average one on that demand.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Explain the causes and mechanisms of the Scramble for Africa. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

Five drivers acted with different functions. Industrial capitalism created the standing demand for raw materials, markets and secure supply, and determined what colonial rule was for. Strategy directed it: the Suez Canal of 1869 and the occupation of Egypt in 1882 made the Nile valley a requirement, giving commercially worthless territory first-order value. Prestige set the pace, since recently unified Germany and Italy sought rank, and when Bismarck backed German claims in 1884 the scramble became openly competitive. Steam, telegraph, prophylaxis and modern firearms supplied capability, never intent. Ideology authorised the expenditure and shaped the institutions built.

The mechanism was the ladder of control. Europeans preferred the cheapest workable form; once rivals claimed exclusivity and Berlin recognised effective occupation, influence ceased to be defensible and every power converted it into occupation - through treaty forms, chartered companies, columns and boundary commissions.

*Scoring note: the separator is the word "function". Scripts that list five motives have answered the enumeration; scripts that assign each driver a job no other can perform, and then supply the ladder mechanism that converted motive into annexation, have answered the question actually set.*

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> "The Berlin Conference gave legality to imperial greed." Comment. (Answer in 150 words)

### Model answer

The statement is accurate about legality and misleading about agency. The conference met from 15 November 1884 to 26 February 1885 with representatives of fourteen countries and no African representation. Its General Act recognised a framework for effective occupation, treated the Congo and Niger as free waters, established freedom of trade in a conventional basin, declared against the slave trade, and recognised Leopold II's Congo Free State - legitimising a private-imperial venture while creating nothing to prevent atrocity.

But it allocated no territory and drew no boundary. Egypt had been occupied in 1882, German claims backed in 1884, and Leopold's treaty campaign begun about 1879; conquest and delimitation continued afterwards.

Its real effect was mechanical: a recognition rule made an unoccupied sphere an invitation. Berlin was decisive as a legitimising rulebook and negligible as a cartographer, accelerating a process already begun.

*Scoring note: the separator is the refusal of the word "divided". Scripts that accept the popular image and then qualify it have conceded the substance; scripts that state what the Act contains, deny the allocation, and then explain why a recognition rule accelerates claims anyway have retained the quotation's force while correcting its history.*

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Was New Imperialism driven more by capitalism than by nationalism? Examine. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The question sets a false alternative: the two drivers answer different questions.

The capitalist case is strong on occurrence and content. Industrial powers wanted raw materials and markets, empire was tied to industrial capitalism, and colonies were reorganised for extraction - an economic fact about what colonial rule was, independent of any minister's stated motive. Hobson in 1902 traced imperialism to surplus capital seeking outlets and to financial capture of policy; Lenin, writing in 1916 and published 1917, made territorial division a necessary stage of monopoly capitalism.

The case is weak on timing and on aggregates. European trade and capital export went overwhelmingly elsewhere; many colonies were unprofitable, rule was often costly, and profits were uneven or concentrated; and industrial demand was decades older than the rush.

Nationalism answers precisely what capitalism cannot. Empire had become a badge of great-power status, and recently unified Germany and Italy sought colonies for rank; when Bismarck backed German claims in 1884 the scramble became openly competitive. Only status competition compounds, since each claim raises the cost of not claiming - which is why the partition is concentrated into two decades. Italy is the decisive case: Eritrea and Somalia acquired, Ethiopia pursued to defeat at Adwa in 1896, with no economic return.

The examined verdict is therefore a division of labour rather than a ranking. Industrial capitalism created the demand and the capacity and determined the extractive content; national rivalry set the pace and selected the moment. Economic motives were central but not exclusive.

*Scoring note: the separator is the explicit refusal of the either/or. Scripts that choose a side must then explain away half the evidence; a script that assigns capitalism the content and nationalism the tempo keeps all of it, and the Italian case is the single item that makes the point unanswerable.*

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> "African societies were subordinated but never merely acted upon." Discuss with reference to the partition of Africa. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

The proposition is correct and requires both halves to be evidenced.

Subordination is not in doubt. About 10 per cent of African territory was under European control in the 1870s and about 90 per cent by 1914, with only Ethiopia and Liberia independent; African voices did not shape the rules, and no African representative attended the conference that divided the continent.

Yet African action shaped what followed, in four forms. Armed defence: Ethiopia defeated Italy at Adwa in 1896, proving conquest contingent rather than inevitable, while the Zulu kingdom, Samori Ture's empire and the Mahdist state fought sustained campaigns - organised states and leaders, not "tribes". Revolt under rule: Maji Maji in 1905-07 rose against labour and taxation demands, two decades after the partition. Negotiation: rulers chose among fighting, seeking the least dangerous protector, playing rivals against each other and accepting subordination for retained internal authority, so colonial terms varied with African bargaining strength. Adaptation and intermediation: European rule depended on African chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors, because the administrative presence was thin - which is how tiny establishments governed vast territories, and why some elites gained.

The qualification is that resistance was uneven, because colonial powers held advantages in logistics, weapons and divided local contexts, and that collaboration is an analytical category, not a moral judgement, since adaptation was constrained rather than free.

Europe imposed; African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered - and the form colonial rule took was shaped by which of these they chose.

*Scoring note: the separator is the fourth form. Most scripts evidence resistance and stop; the higher band belongs to scripts that treat intermediation and adaptation as agency, because those explain how the colonial state actually functioned and where later leadership came from.*

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Analyse the economic and social consequences of New Imperialism in Africa. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Colonial rule did not merely take from African societies; it reorganised them, and the engine was fiscal.

A tax payable in cash, assessed per hut or adult male and collected through chiefs, was imposed on households that used little cash. They could obtain it only by growing an export crop, by selling labour, or by working in lieu of tax - so taxation recruited labour without any order to work being issued. Land was simultaneously constrained: alienated to settlers, as on the Kenyan highland plateau reserved for whites; granted to concession companies; or declared vacant and taken as state property, expropriation by definition.

The economic consequence was reorientation. Colonies were reorganised for extraction; railways ran producer-to-port rather than between African regions; territories came to depend on one or two commodities; processing remained elsewhere - Nyasaland had not one industrial factory at British withdrawal.

The social consequences were deeper. Male labour migration shifted farming and household responsibility onto women, while colonial law and mission institutions redefined marriage and property; in the developed systems men lived in barracks away from their families, so rural households subsidised the wage. Administration recognised and sometimes invented chiefs, hardening fluid identities into fixed categories. Conquest, forced labour, movement and disrupted food systems raised mortality in several regions - a finding that must stay qualitative, since no figures are sourced.

Maji Maji of 1905-07, against labour and taxation demands, measures the burden. Colonial rule reorganised African economies for extraction and African politics for control.

*Scoring note: the separator is the household paragraph. The land, labour and export material appears in most scripts; the gendered restructuring, the rural subsidy of the migrant wage and the administrative fixing of identity are recorded as the dimensions most answers omit, and they are where a 20-mark script earns its top band.*

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Analyse the long-term effects of the Scramble for Africa on African society and politics. (Answer in 250 words)

### Model answer

Four inheritances outlasted the empires that created them.

Territorial: boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities, and many frontiers grouped communities with weak shared political identity - the most cited legacy, and the origin of later interstate and ethnic tension. Economic: extractive structures produced commodity dependence that outlasted empire, with producer-to-port transport, no processing and continuing reliance on western markets and investment. Social: administration recognised and sometimes invented chiefs, hardening fluid identities into fixed administrative categories - a direct mechanism from colonial rule to later ethnic politics. Ideological: a hardened racial hierarchy, constitutionalised where settlers ruled, as in the Union of South Africa of 1910; and, as its counterpart, the violent memory from which anti-colonial nationalism drew strength.

The decisive question is why independent states kept frontiers they condemned. Because every alternative was worse: reopening one border legitimises reopening all, and no state's frontiers would survive general revision. The evidence is behavioural, not documentary - the Organization of African Unity of 1963 became the forum for African conflicts; neither the UN, the Commonwealth nor the OAU would support Biafra's secession; Katanga's secession was reversed with UN troops; and separation occurred only by agreement, as when Singapore left Malaysia in 1965.

The cost was internal war: Nigeria's unity was preserved only through a conflict in which Biafra lost more civilians to disease and starvation than troops killed fighting.

The deepest legacy was structural, not territorial; the borders merely made it harder to escape - though inheritance constrains rather than determines.

*Scoring note: the separator is the preservation argument. Every script states that the borders were arbitrary; almost none explains why they were kept, and a script that supplies the collective-security reasoning, the behavioural evidence and the cost - while claiming no OAU document - is writing political analysis rather than lament.*

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

| # | The error | Why it costs marks | The correction | Taught in |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Using "imperialism" and "colonialism" interchangeably | Causes answers get written for consequences questions | Imperialism is the building of empire by seizing territory; colonialism is the administrative form - settlement, land, labour, law, revenue | Lesson 1 |
| 2a | Dating the Transvaal and Orange Free State to the Great Trek years | Merges a migration with a later recognition of statehood | Great Trek (1835-40); later independent republics recognised 1852 and 1854 | Lessons 2, 5, 13 |
| 2 | Dating European interest in Africa from 1880 | Makes the topic's actual claim invisible | 1880 dates the change in the form of the claim, not first contact | Lesson 1 |
| 3 | Varying or failing to attribute the 10-per-cent and 90-per-cent figures | Breaches an explicit factual-risk control | About 10 per cent in the 1870s, about 90 per cent by 1914, attributed | Lesson 1 |
| 4 | Reading the 90-per-cent figure as effective administration | Overstates colonial capacity and hides the post-partition violence | It measures claims recognised among Europeans; administration lagged by decades | Lessons 1, 12 |
| 5 | Writing that Africa lacked political organisation | Restates the imperial justification as a finding | Four political forms, each producing a different conquest method and afterlife | Lesson 2 |
| 6 | Using "tribe" as a natural unit | Erases the administrative mechanism that created it | Administration recognised and sometimes invented chiefs, hardening fluid identities | Lessons 2, 15, 19 |
| 7 | Presenting Africa as isolated before European contact | Makes the commercial displacement of African traders inexplicable | Trans-Saharan, Sudanic, Indian Ocean and Atlantic systems all preceded partition | Lesson 2 |
| 8 | Improvising figures for the Atlantic slave trade | Breaches a recorded bounded gap | No source-verified triangular-trade or abolition evidence is held | Lesson 2 |
| 9 | Treating "empire" as identical with "colony" | Empties the pre-1880 period of imperialism | Empire is control; the ladder runs from trade to colony | Lesson 3 |
| 10 | Using "sphere of influence" as though it bound African authorities | Misstates what the instrument does | It binds only the European signatories | Lesson 3 |
| 11 | Assuming annexation implies profitability | The unprofitable-colony evidence then appears to refute the answer | The scarce good was exclusivity; insurance is bought against a risk, not for a return | Lessons 3, 4 |
| 12 | Presenting chartered companies as commercial bodies | Loses the delegated-sovereignty pathology | They held governing and revenue powers by charter; profit and sovereignty overlapped | Lessons 3, 10, 12 |
| 13 | Writing "economic causes" as one undifferentiated motive | Cannot concede one strand and retain another | Three distinct arguments: inputs, outlets, capital export | Lesson 4 |
| 13a | Treating Lenin's *Imperialism, the Highest Stage of Capitalism* as first published in the year it was written | Conflates composition with publication | Written 1916, first published 1917 | Lessons 4, 20 |
| 14 | Attributing to Hobson the claim that imperialism is inevitable | Reverses his argument and confuses him with Lenin | Hobson: a remediable policy with redistribution as remedy. Lenin: a necessary stage ending in redivision | Lessons 4, 20 |
| 15 | Attributing statistics to Hobson, Lenin, Schumpeter or Gallagher-Robinson | Asserts what is not held here | Positions with arguments only; no statistic is attributed to any of them | Lessons 4, 20 |
| 16 | Ignoring the aggregate-flow objection to the economic thesis | Leaves the answer refutable by an obvious fact | Concede it, then rebuild on supply security, concentrated interests and extractive structure | Lesson 4 |
| 17 | Describing Egypt after 1882 as a British colony | Misnames the instrument | A veiled occupation under retained Khedivial and Ottoman forms | Lesson 5 |
| 18 | Presenting 1882 as a planned strategic seizure | Omits the recorded caution and produces a masterplan narrative | Strategy often followed financial entanglement rather than preceding it | Lesson 5 |
| 19 | Omitting the Cape as the second strategic axis | Makes southern Africa unexplainable | Cape captured 1795, confirmed British 1814; the pre-canal route to India | Lesson 5 |
| 20 | Explaining Germany's 1884 entry by raw-material needs | Those needs did not change between 1880 and 1884 | Commercial occasion, domestic incentive, diplomatic opportunity, and the recognition race | Lesson 6 |
| 21 | Treating prestige as a vague mood | Makes it unfalsifiable and easily dismissed | It explains timing and speed, predicts unprofitable acquisition, and compounds | Lesson 6 |
| 22 | Omitting the caution that prestige cannot explain the economic reorganisation | Overclaims a bounded driver | State the boundary; it is recorded | Lesson 6 |
| 23 | Omitting Italy from a causes answer | Discards the clearest prestige case | Eritrea and Somalia acquired; Ethiopia pursued to defeat at Adwa | Lessons 6, 13 |
| 24 | Presenting superior technology as the cause of the partition | Confuses capability with intent | Technology converts desire into feasibility; it explains capability, never intent | Lesson 7 |
| 25 | Forgetting medicine among the enabling changes | Loses the least glamorous and most decisive one | Antimalarial prophylaxis made inland administration survivable | Lesson 7 |
| 26 | Implying African defeat was technologically inevitable | Makes Adwa inexplicable and erases agency | The gap depended on arms supply, terrain, command unity and sequencing | Lessons 7, 14 |
| 27 | Reporting the civilising mission neutrally | Reproduces the imperial claim as a finding | Test stated motive against recorded conduct; that comparison is the analysis | Lesson 8 |
| 28 | Dismissing the civilising mission as pure propaganda | Loses the recorded point that it did real work in policy | It legitimated, partially constrained, and decisively shaped the form of rule | Lesson 8 |
| 29 | Calling Berlin a humanitarian anti-slavery conference | Breaches an explicit correction | Anti-slavery language existed, but rivalry and territorial claims were central | Lessons 8, 11 |
| 30 | Treating abolition as disproving self-interest | Ignores what the same powers then did | The powers that proclaimed abolition proceeded to impose forced labour | Lessons 8, 17 |
| 31 | Presenting racial hierarchy as private prejudice | Misses why it was politically decisive | It was presented as a scientific finding, which removed it from argument | Lesson 8 |
| 32 | Writing five equal paragraphs and calling it multi-causal | A list asserts presence; an explanation assigns function | Give each driver a job with a verb, and run the removal test | Lesson 9 |
| 33 | Ranking causes without specifying what is being explained | Makes the ranking arbitrary | Occurrence favours economics; timing favours rivalry; selection favours strategy | Lesson 9 |
| 34 | Concluding with "a combination of complex factors" | Asserts complexity instead of analysing it | Name the couplings: financial entanglement into strategy; cheapness into prestige | Lesson 9 |
| 35 | Treating the Congo Free State as a Belgian colony from 1885 | Misstates the constitutional form and the argument | A personal sovereignty of Leopold II; Belgian annexation only in 1908 | Lesson 10 |
| 36 | Supplying casualty figures for Congo Free State rule | Breaches an explicit prohibition | None is sourced; state the system and refuse the number | Lessons 10, 18 |
| 37 | Explaining the Congo Free State by Leopold's ambition alone | Misses why the powers recognised it | A claimant too weak to threaten anyone was the cheapest way to exclude a rival | Lesson 10 |
| 38 | Presenting Berlin as the cause of the Congo situation | Reverses the sequence | The Congo dispute was the cause of Berlin | Lessons 10, 11 |
| 39 | Writing that Berlin partitioned Africa or drew the borders | Breaches an explicit control | It created rules and legitimacy; conquest and delimitation continued afterward | Lesson 11 |
| 40 | Giving any figure other than fourteen participants, or any other dates | Breaches an explicit control | Fourteen countries; 15 November 1884 to 26 February 1885 | Lesson 11 |
| 41 | Saying Berlin started the scramble | Contradicts the chronology | Egypt 1882; German claims 1884; Leopold's campaign from about 1879 | Lesson 11 |
| 42 | Omitting the absence of African representation | Loses the key to the instrument's content | African voices did not shape the rules; every provision protects Europeans from Europeans | Lesson 11 |
| 43 | Describing effective occupation without noting its narrow formal scope | Misses a precision mark | As adopted it applied to new coastal acquisitions with notification; the doctrine grew wider | Lesson 11 |
| 44 | Omitting the mechanism by which a recognition rule accelerates claims | Produces description instead of explanation | An unoccupied sphere becomes an invitation rather than a holding | Lessons 3, 11 |
| 45 | Treating the partition as completed in 1885 | Conceals the post-partition violence | Conversion ran into the twentieth century through five stages | Lesson 12 |
| 46 | Describing treaties as agreements between equals | Ignores three specific defects | Capacity to sign, meaning of what was signed, and a European audience | Lesson 12 |
| 47 | Forgetting that colonial armies were overwhelmingly African | Misdescribes how conquest was executed | African soldiers under European officers with modern firearms | Lessons 12, 15 |
| 48 | Explaining border arbitrariness by malice | Weaker than the available mechanism | Lines agreed between governments describing territory neither had visited | Lessons 12, 19 |
| 49 | Misassigning holdings in the partition table | A directly checkable factual error | Britain, France, Belgium, Germany, Portugal, Italy - as recorded | Lesson 12 |
| 50 | Treating Adwa as typical of African military capacity | Contradicts the recorded caution | It proves contingency and was exceptional; state both | Lessons 13, 14 |
| 51 | Describing Fashoda without the geometry | Loses the only analytically load-bearing element | A British longitudinal axis crossing a French latitudinal one | Lesson 13 |
| 52 | Supplying casualty figures for Omdurman, Adwa or the South African War | Asserts what is not held | Only the camp proportion the core source states, attributed as such | Lesson 13 |
| 53 | Extending the camp figure to populations for which no figure is held | Invents evidence | No separate figure is held for African camps in that war | Lesson 13 |
| 54 | Treating the South African War as a war against Africans | Misstates the belligerents and hides the settlement's exclusion | Fought principally between Europeans; the Union of 1910 excluded an African majority | Lesson 13 |
| 55 | Drifting from Morocco into the alliance system and the July crisis | Trespasses on another topic | Stop at the African outcomes of 1905-06 and 1911 | Lesson 13 |
| 56 | Merging resistance at conquest with revolt under rule | Destroys both the analysis and the chronology | Maji Maji, 1905-07, is a response to administration, not to invasion | Lesson 14 |
| 57 | Writing generally about African resistance without naming polities | Contradicts the record's insistence on organised states | Ethiopia under Menelik II; the Zulu kingdom; Samori Ture; the Mahdist state | Lesson 14 |
| 58 | Criticising African polities for failing to unite | Presupposes an institution that did not exist | Simultaneous continental resistance had no possible organiser; European states did not unite either | Lessons 14, 20 |
| 59 | Generalising the term genocide to all colonial violence | Breaches an explicit instruction and weakens the term | Name the genocide of the Herero and Nama precisely | Lessons 14, 18 |
| 60 | Attributing atrocity to nations or peoples | Converts a historical claim into a collective charge | Attribute to named regimes, administrations, commands, companies and policies | Lesson 18 |
| 61 | Using "collaborator" as a moral verdict | Breaches the recorded analytical framing | Collaboration is an analytical category; adaptation was constrained, not free | Lesson 15 |
| 62 | Omitting the arithmetic of the thin colonial state | Leaves the dependence on intermediaries unexplained | Tiny European establishments; enormous tasks; five intermediary roles | Lesson 15 |
| 63 | Presenting the colonial origins of the educated class as a benefit | Ignores that administrations tried to prevent it | Belgian policy denied advanced education; seventeen graduates at independence | Lessons 15, 16 |
| 64 | Defining direct and indirect rule and stopping | Describes without analysing | Say what followed from the difference, and in which domain | Lesson 16 |
| 65 | Treating the models as national philosophies | Replaces structure with character | Fiscal capacity, existing African structures, climate, economic type | Lesson 16 |
| 66 | Omitting the settler variable | Misses the axis that did most work | West Africa's straightforward transfer against the bitterness where settlers were entrenched | Lesson 16 |
| 67 | Writing that imperialism was one uniform model | Explicitly warned against | Distinguish types of control carefully | Lessons 3, 16 |
| 68 | Importing Indian revenue, famine or statute material | Breaches the boundary | Structural comparison only; cross-read the Indian topics | Lessons 16, 20 |
| 69 | Saying colonies were "exploited" without a mechanism | Assertion rather than analysis | The cash-tax engine: tax, then export crop or wage labour or labour in lieu | Lesson 17 |
| 70 | Treating forced labour as a single practice | Loses the gradation | Labour in lieu of tax; statutory obligations; penal contract; quota compulsion | Lesson 17 |
| 71 | Omitting household and gender consequences | Discards the dimension recorded as most often missed | Responsibility shifted onto women; law and missions redefined marriage and property | Lesson 17 |
| 72 | Supplying mortality, tax, wage or output figures | Breaches the qualitative instruction | Keep health and demography qualitative; no figures are sourced | Lesson 17 |
| 73 | Claiming African living standards rose or fell | No series exists here to support either | State mechanisms and directions; refuse magnitudes | Lesson 17 |
| 74 | Treating railways as evidence of development | Ignores their orientation | Producer-to-port, not between African regions | Lessons 7, 17 |
| 75 | Striking a single balance of colonial costs and benefits | The evidence cannot support a net figure | Assess extent separately: thinly spread benefits; extractive orientation; new coercion | Lesson 17 |
| 76 | Confessing source bias in a closing sentence | Explicitly warned against | Build the source critique into the analysis through its five defects | Lesson 18 |
| 77 | Naming colonial officials, reports or historiographical schools in the source critique | Breaches an explicit control | The critique is built only from evidence already held | Lesson 18 |
| 78 | Producing a list of atrocities instead of a systemic account | Weaker analytically and easier to answer | Four categories with their logics: conquest, administrative, extractive, exterminatory | Lesson 18 |
| 79 | Stating that colonial borders were arbitrary and stopping | The most common and least rewarded observation in the topic | Explain why independent states nonetheless kept them | Lesson 19 |
| 80 | Claiming a formal OAU border-inviolability resolution | Breaches an explicit control | The evidence held is behavioural; state the mechanism, not a document | Lesson 19 |
| 81 | Treating the inheritance as deterministic | Contradicts the recorded control and erases post-colonial agency | Inheritance sets the starting position and the constraints, not the outcome | Lesson 19 |
| 82 | Omitting the cost of the border-preservation choice | Makes the argument admiring rather than honest | Territorial stability was bought at the price of internal wars | Lesson 19 |
| 83 | Using the current linkage as historical evidence | The record confines it to legacy and reparatory justice | It does not verify Berlin Conference history | Lesson 19 |
| 84 | Naming theorists without stating what they argued | Creates an expectation the answer fails | Four sentences each: claim, argument, strongest objection, residual value | Lesson 20 |
| 85 | Applying Gallagher and Robinson's account to every power | It is drawn substantially from British experience | State the limitation when using the peripheral explanation | Lesson 20 |
| 86 | Declaring a historiographical debate resolved | The standard accounts supply syntheses, not verdicts | State the synthesis and mark what the evidence cannot settle | Lesson 20 |
| 87 | Answering only the causes half of the syllabus clause | The clause names colonisation **and** its effect on society | Reserve words for land, labour, households, authority, identity and borders | Lesson 21 |
| 88 | Narrating a neighbouring topic's material | Loses time and gains nothing | Five hand-offs, one clause each | Lesson 21 |
