# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

⚠️ Every date below is held by a named source. None is asserted that is not, and the provenance of each is set out in the source ledger.

| Date | Event | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ 1795 | The Cape captured by Britain during the French Revolutionary Wars | The southern strategic axis begins; the pre-canal route to India |
| ✅ 1814 | The peace settlement confirms the Cape as British | Permanence of the southern position |
| ✅ 1835-40 | The Great Trek; Boer migration northwards from Cape Colony; settlement in Natal | The movement that produced the inland Boer communities |
| ✅ 1838 | Emancipation throughout the British Empire, a recorded grievance of the Boer farmers | Connects abolition to settler politics in southern Africa |
| ✅ 1852 and 1854 | The later independent republics recognised - the Transvaal, or South African Republic, by the Sand River Convention of 1852, and the Orange Free State by the Bloemfontein Convention of 1854 | Creates the settler republics whose later conflict produces the costliest imperial war of the period |
| ✅ 1869 | The Suez Canal opened | Africa and the Asian routes gain strategic weight; Egypt becomes the artery of an empire |
| ✅ 1870s | European control in Africa still limited mainly to coasts; about **10 per cent** of African territory under European control | The baseline against which the partition is measured |
| ✅ 1875 | Britain purchases the Khedive's canal shares | A direct financial stake in the waterway |
| ✅ 1876 | The Brussels geographical conference; the International African Association formed | The humanitarian and scientific vehicle for a private empire |
| ✅ from 1879 | Stanley's station-building and treaty campaign in the Congo basin; Brazza's rival French treaties | The paper claim that makes the basin an international question |
| ✅ 1879 | The Anglo-Zulu War, with a severe early British defeat before the kingdom is overcome | Organised African states could inflict serious defeats |
| ✅ 1882 | Britain occupies Egypt | Strategic-financial imperialism becomes direct rule; the Nile valley becomes a requirement |
| ✅ 1884 | The Anglo-Portuguese treaty over the Congo mouth is abandoned | Bilateral settlement shown to be impossible |
| ✅ 1884 | Bismarck backs German claims in Africa | The scramble becomes openly competitive |
| ✅ 15 Nov 1884 - 26 Feb 1885 | The Berlin Conference; representatives of **fourteen** countries; no African representation | The General Act recognises a framework for effective occupation and accelerates later claims |
| ✅ 1885 | The Congo Free State recognised under Leopold II | Sovereignty and profit held by one hand; a private-imperial venture legitimised |
| ✅ 1886, 1888, 1889 | The Royal Niger Company, the Imperial British East Africa Company and the British South Africa Company chartered | Delegated sovereignty as the instrument of conversion |
| ✅ 1890 | Portugal's rose-coloured map abandoned after the British ultimatum; the Anglo-German agreement settles East African boundaries and transfers Heligoland | African territory as diplomatic currency |
| ✅ 1895-96 | The failed Jameson Raid into the Transvaal | Intensifies the confrontation preceding the South African War |
| ✅ 1896 | Ethiopia defeats Italy at Adwa, ✅ on 1 March, under Menelik II | African resistance could succeed; conquest was contingent, not inevitable |
| ✅ 1898 | Marchand at Fashoda in July; Omdurman on 2 September; French withdrawal in November; spheres fixed by declaration in 1899 | The procedural peace among Europeans holds, at its limit |
| ✅ 1898 | Samori Ture captured after roughly two decades of resistance | The end of the longest sustained West African defence |
| ✅ 1899-1902 | The Boer War; Britain defeats the Transvaal and the Orange Free State | The costliest imperial war of the period, fought principally between Europeans in Africa |
| ✅ 1904 | The Entente Cordiale: France recognises Britain's position in Egypt; Britain approves a possible French takeover of Morocco | The Fashoda quarrel converted into a colonial bargain |
| ✅ 1904 | Casement's consular report on the Congo; the Congo Reform Association founded; E. D. Morel's campaign | An ideology that authorised a venture becomes the standard by which it is condemned |
| ✅ 1904-08 | The genocide of the Herero and Nama, German South West Africa | The strongest single evidence against the civilising-mission claim |
| ✅ 1905-06 | The Moroccan Crisis; the Algeciras conference in January 1906; a serious diplomatic defeat for Germany | African questions become European alignment tests |
| ✅ 1905-07 | The Maji Maji uprising, German East Africa, against labour and taxation demands | Resistance continued after conquest, against administration |
| ✅ 1908 | Belgium annexes the Congo Free State, creating the Belgian Congo | The concessionary model collapses into state administration |
| ✅ 1910 | The Union of South Africa formed from the Transvaal, the Orange Free State, Cape Colony and Natal | A settlement between two European communities over an African majority of about 70 per cent |
| ✅ 1911 | The Agadir Crisis; Germany recognises the French protectorate over Morocco for two strips of the French Congo | The last unclaimed area allocated; African territory as a counter |
| ✅ 1914 | About **90 per cent** of African territory under European control; only **Ethiopia and Liberia** independent | The partition benchmark |
| ✅ 1922 | ✅ Lugard's *The Dual Mandate in British Tropical Africa* published | The classic statement of indirect rule and trusteeship |
| ✅ 1963 | The Organization of African Unity founded | The forum whose behaviour supplies the border-preservation evidence - handed to the decolonisation topic |

## The causal engine

```text
   STANDING CONDITION (STRUCTURAL)
   industrial economies requiring inputs, outlets and secure supply
   -> present for decades before the partition; explains WHY AT ALL
                              |
   DIRECTIONAL CONDITION
   the route to India: Suez 1869 -> Egyptian debt -> occupation 1882
   -> the Nile becomes a requirement; commercially worthless
      territory acquires first-order value; France is redirected
                              |
   PROXIMATE TRIGGER
   the entry of two newly unified states seeking rank;
   Bismarck backs German claims, 1884
   -> the scramble becomes OPENLY COMPETITIVE
                              |
   THE AMPLIFIER (the only one that compounds)
   each claim raises the expected cost of NOT claiming
   -> no power can decline without appearing to decline
                              |
   THE CRISIS THAT FORCED A RULEBOOK
   one river, one mouth, five interested parties, a weak claimant
   -> BERLIN, 15 Nov 1884 - 26 Feb 1885
   -> EFFECTIVE OCCUPATION recognised
                              |
   THE MECHANISM THAT DID THE WORK
   a recognition rule converts an UNOCCUPIED SPHERE from a holding
   into an INVITATION
   -> influence ceases to be defensible -> everyone climbs the ladder
                              |
   PERMISSIVE CONDITIONS (throughout)
   quinine prophylaxis | river steamer and railway | telegraph |
   breech-loader, magazine rifle, machine gun + restricted arms sales
   -> survive, move, coordinate, win: conquest becomes cheap
                              |
   LEGITIMATING CONDITIONS (throughout)
   civilising mission | anti-slavery | racial science as "finding"
   -> expenditure authorised; institutions designed;
      opposition characterised as sentimentality
                              |
   ===================== EXECUTION, 1885-1902 =====================
   treaty form -> bilateral convention -> company or column ->
   administration -> boundary commission
   -> boundaries rarely follow ethnic or political realities
                              |
   ===================== CONTENT OF RULE =========================
   cash tax -> export crop OR wage labour OR labour in lieu
   -> land alienated, conceded or declared vacant
   -> male absence -> responsibility onto women
   -> producer-to-port transport, one or two commodities
   -> coercion: conquest / administrative / extractive / exterminatory
                              |
   ===================== INHERITANCE =============================
   artificial frontiers | extractive structures |
   administratively fixed identities | a racial order
   + violent memory -> anti-colonial nationalism (handed over)
                              |
   BORDERS KEPT: a rule of revision would have had no stopping point
```

## The five drivers in one table

| Driver | Causal type | The job only it performs | Strongest evidence | Its recorded limit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Economic** | Structural | Why at all, and what colonial rule was for | ✅ Industrial powers wanted raw materials and markets; ✅ colonies reorganised for extraction | ✅ Not all colonies were profitable; rule could be costly; profits concentrated |
| **Strategic** | Directional | Which territories, and in what order | ✅ Suez 1869; ✅ Egypt 1882; ✅ sea routes and rival empires mattered | ✅ Strategy often followed financial entanglement rather than preceding it |
| **Prestige and rivalry** | Proximate | Timing and speed; the compounding of claims | ✅ Germany and Italy sought colonies; ✅ Bismarck backs German claims, 1884 | ✅ Prestige alone cannot explain the economic reorganisation that followed |
| **Technology and medicine** | Permissive | Capability, cheapness and the one-sidedness of battle | ✅ Steam transport, improved weapons and medicines increased European reach | ✅ Technology explains capability, never intent |
| **Ideology** | Legitimating | Authorisation at home; the form of rule | ✅ Civilising-mission and racial rhetoric justified expansion; ✅ it shaped the form of rule | ✅ Neither sincere motive nor pure cover; ✅ moral language often masked exploitation |

## Berlin: what it did and did not do

| Berlin **did** | ✅ Evidence | Berlin **did not** | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|---|
| Set a recognition rule | ✅ The General Act recognised a framework for **effective occupation** | Draw the actual borders on one day | ✅ Conquest and delimitation continued afterward |
| Convene the interested powers | ✅ Met 15 Nov 1884 - 26 Feb 1885; representatives from **fourteen** countries | Include African representation | ✅ African voices did not shape the rules |
| Legitimise a private-imperial venture | ✅ The Congo Free State recognised under **Leopold II** | Prevent atrocity | ✅ Colonial rule often meant coercion and atrocity |
| Open two rivers and a basin to commerce | ✅ The Congo and Niger discussed as free waters; ✅ commerce and empire were intertwined | Create any obligation enforceable by an African party | ⚠️ Every provision protects European interests against other Europeans |

## African agency: the four verbs in one table

| Verb | Form | ✅ Named evidence | Analytical significance | Caution |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Resisted** | Successful armed defence | ✅ Ethiopia at **Adwa, 1896** | Conquest was contingent, not inevitable | ⚠️ Exceptional; most resistance was defeated |
| **Resisted** | Sustained armed defence | ✅ **Zulu** resistance; ✅ **Samori Ture**; ✅ the **Mahdist state** to 1898 | Organised states and leaders, not "tribes" | ⚠️ Different polities resisted for different reasons |
| **Resisted** | Mass revolt under rule | ✅ **Maji Maji, 1905-07**, against labour and taxation demands | Resistance continued after conquest, against administration | ⚠️ Do not give casualty figures |
| **Negotiated** | Treaty-seeking and the use of rivalry | ⚠️ Four strategic options: fight; least dangerous protector; play rivals; subordination for retained authority | Colonial terms varied with African bargaining strength | ⚠️ The window of leverage was brief |
| **Adapted** | Intermediation | ✅ Rule depended on African **chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors** | How tiny establishments governed vast territories; why some elites gained | ⚠️ An analytical category, not a moral judgement |
| **Adapted** | Economic and institutional | ✅ Cash crops, migrant labour, mission schooling, new legal forums, **used where possible** | Explains where later nationalist leadership came from | ⚠️ Constrained, not free choice |
| **Suffered** | The extremity of response | ✅ The **genocide of the Herero and Nama, 1904-08** | The strongest single evidence against the civilising-mission claim | ⚠️ Name it precisely; no casualty figures; no collective attribution |

## The four models of rule, side by side

| | Direct rule / assimilation | Indirect rule | Concessionary rule | Settler colony |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Logic** | The colony as an extension of the metropolitan state | Govern through existing authority under supervision | The territory pays for its own conquest | Permanent settlement secures the territory |
| **Requires** | A large European cadre and a trained African subordinate service | An authority to rule through | Companies willing to bear the cost for extraction rights | A resident European population with land and capital |
| **Characteristic pathology** | The gap between the assimilationist promise and the few admitted | ✅ Chiefs **recognised and sometimes invented**; "custom" fixed administratively | ✅ **Profit and sovereignty overlapped**; no regulator | A settler lobby the metropole cannot control |
| **Recorded instance** | ⚠️ France; Portugal in a harsher form | ✅ Lugard; Northern Nigeria; *The Dual Mandate*, 1922 | ✅ The Congo Free State; equatorial concession zones | ✅ Kenya's highland reservation; ✅ the Union of South Africa, 1910 |
| **Long-run signature** | A metropolitan-educated elite | Entrenched chiefly authority and fixed ethnic categories | Extraction embedded in the revenue system | ✅ The bitterest later confrontation, against ✅ West Africa's comparatively straightforward transfer |

## Argument map: how far was the Berlin Conference responsible?

```text
   CLAIM: "Berlin partitioned Africa"
        |
        +-- SUPPORT: the sharpest phase of claiming follows the conference
        |            fourteen powers met and produced a binding instrument
        |            the Congo Free State emerged directly from it
        |
        +-- REFUTATION (decisive)
        |     no territory was allocated; no colonial boundary was drawn
        |     Egypt occupied 1882; German claims backed 1884;
        |     Leopold's campaign from about 1879 -- ALL PRECEDE IT
        |     conquest and delimitation continued afterward
        |
        +-- THE REPLACEMENT CLAIM (what actually happened)
        |     a RECOGNITION RULE changes the cost of NOT occupying
        |     -> an unoccupied sphere becomes an invitation
        |     -> every power converts influence into occupation
        |     -> the partition becomes fast, competitive and
        |        settleable WITHOUT WAR AMONG EUROPEANS
        |
        +-- THE MORAL COUNTERPART (one clause, not omitted)
        |     the peace among Europeans was purchased by transferring
        |     the violence to Africa
        |
        +-- VERDICT
              "decisive as a legitimising rulebook and negligible as
               a cartographer"
```

## Argument map: was the partition economically driven?

```text
   PROPOSITION: industrial capitalism caused the Scramble for Africa
        |
   FOR                                    AGAINST
   -- industrial powers wanted raw        -- trade and capital export went
      materials and markets                  overwhelmingly elsewhere
   -- empire was tied to industrial       -- many colonies were unprofitable;
      capitalism                             rule could be costly; profits
   -- colonies were REORGANISED FOR          were uneven and concentrated
      EXTRACTION (the content of rule)    -- industrial demand predates the
   -- the partition's most contested         rush by decades
      zones were the mineral and          -- decision-makers frequently gave
      rubber-and-ivory regions               strategic or prestige reasons
        |                                          |
        +---------------------+--------------------+
                              |
   THE MISSPECIFIED TEST
   aggregate flows measure WHERE MONEY WENT;
   the imperial question is WHERE ORGANISED INTERESTS WANTED
   POLITICAL SECURITY -- a small share of investment can be a very
   large share of the pressure on a colonial ministry
                              |
   THE DECISIVE DISTINCTION
   ECONOMIC MOTIVE (poorly evidenced) vs ECONOMIC STRUCTURE (well evidenced)
   whatever brought a territory under control, it was then reorganised
   for extraction
                              |
   VERDICT
   "industrial capitalism created the demand and the capacity and
    determined what colonial rule was for; great-power rivalry
    determined when and how fast the demand became territory"
   -> economic motives were CENTRAL BUT NOT EXCLUSIVE
```

## Argument map: did Africans have agency?

```text
   WEAK FORM (what most scripts write)
   "Africans resisted heroically but were defeated."
        -> true, thin, and still a story in which Africa only reacts
        |
   THE RECORDED FORMULA
   NOT: "Europe acted -> Africa received"
   BUT: "Europe imposed + African societies RESISTED, NEGOTIATED,
         ADAPTED and SUFFERED"
        |
   WHAT EACH VERB PROVES
   RESISTED   -> African states had armies, command, logistics and
                 arms-acquisition strategies: Adwa 1896 proves the
                 outcome was contingent
   NEGOTIATED -> colonial TERMS varied with African bargaining
                 strength: this is African action shaping European
                 institutions
   ADAPTED    -> the colonial state was ASSEMBLED OUT OF African
                 personnel; and schooling, employment and exclusion
                 produced the class that later ended colonial rule
   SUFFERED   -> the extremity of colonial response is itself
                 evidence of what resistance compelled
        |
   THE DISCIPLINE THAT KEEPS IT HONEST
   agency = attributable action under coercion, NOT freedom
   collaboration = an analytical category, NOT a moral judgement
   adaptation = constrained, NOT free choice
        |
   VERDICT
   "African societies were subordinated but never merely acted upon,
    and the pattern of European rule was shaped by which of these
    they chose"
```

## Answer-writing spine

```text
   STEP 1  IDENTIFY THE EXPLANANDUM
           causes? | mechanism? | consequences? | agency? | borders?
   STEP 2  ANCHOR  (2 sentences)
           definition + "about 10 per cent in the 1870s, about 90 per
           cent by 1914, only Ethiopia and Liberia independent"
   STEP 3  THESIS  (1 sentence, chosen from four)
           multi-causal | Berlin | consequences | agency
   STEP 4  BODY    (structure DICTATED by the thesis)
           drivers-with-jobs | did/did-not/mechanism |
           domain-by-domain | four verbs
   STEP 5  ONE INTERACTION OR MECHANISM PARAGRAPH
           the highest-value discretionary content available
   STEP 6  HAND OFF  (1 clause each, only where needed)
           industrialisation | unifications | 1914 | decolonisation | India
   STEP 7  VERDICT  (1-2 sentences, from the matching scaffold)
   STEP 8  CONTROL SWEEP
           fourteen countries | exact dates | two percentages attributed |
           no casualty figures | no "Berlin partitioned Africa" |
           no OAU document | no collective attribution
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The category and the measurement

- ✅ Imperialism is the building up of an empire by seizing territory overseas; ✅ a great burst of imperialist expansion followed **1880**; ✅ most of Africa was taken over in what became known as the **Scramble for Africa**, the idea behind it being mainly to obtain **new markets and new sources of raw materials**.
- ⚠️ Colonialism is the administrative form - settlement, land, labour, law, revenue - and is not a synonym for imperialism.
- ✅ **1870s: about 10 per cent** of African territory under European control; ✅ **1914: about 90 per cent**. Attribute the figures; never vary them. ✅ Only **Ethiopia and Liberia** independent in 1914.
- ✅ In the 1870s European control was **limited mainly to coasts**.
- ⚠️ Four senses of "new": form (inland political control), pace (one generation), competitors (industrial states including two new ones), rules (effective occupation). ⚠️ Three overstatements to refuse: that interest began after 1870; that motives were unprecedented; that the partition was one plan.
- ⚠️ The figures measure **claims**, not working administration.

## 2. The continent before the partition

- ⚠️ Four political forms: centralised states with armies; trading states and merchant networks; decentralised and segmentary societies; pastoral and mobile societies. Each produced a different conquest method and a different colonial afterlife.
- ✅ Resistance came from **organised states and leaders, not "tribes"**; ✅ different polities resisted for different reasons.
- ⚠️ Africa was already connected: trans-Saharan caravan trade, Sudanic Islamic states, the Indian Ocean and Swahili commercial world, and the Atlantic system and its aftermath. ✅ **Bounded gap:** no source-verified triangular-trade or abolition evidence is held; improvise no figures.
- ⚠️ Five obstacles to inland penetration before 1870: disease; African political strength; transport; military parity; absence of commercial incentive.
- ✅ The southern sequence: Cape captured **1795**, confirmed British **1814**; emancipation throughout the Empire **1838**; the **Great Trek (1835-40)** and settlement in Natal, with the **later independent republics recognised 1852 and 1854** - the Transvaal by the Sand River Convention of 1852 and the Orange Free State by the Bloemfontein Convention of 1854.
- ⚠️ Nineteenth-century Africa was already changing: Sudanic state-building; southern African state formation; Ethiopian imperial reconstruction under Menelik II; Egyptian state modernisation and borrowing.
- ⚠️ Assert **no** population, revenue, army-size or trade-volume figure for any African polity.

## 3. The ladder of control

- ⚠️ Six rungs: trade under African terms; informal empire; sphere of influence; chartered company; protectorate; colony. Costs rise; only the upper rungs exclude a rival.
- ✅ Distinguish types of control carefully: ✅ direct rule on one view, ✅ informal leverage and chartered-company power on the other.
- ⚠️ A treaty of protection binds the signatory; a sphere of influence binds only the European signatories; a colony binds the metropolitan budget.
- ⚠️ Cheap instruments are unstable: protectorates drift into administration; companies fail into state control.
- ⚠️ Four failure conditions for informal empire: a rival claims exclusivity; the local partner collapses; the frontier generates commitments; a recognition rule makes non-occupation unsafe.
- ⚠️ The scarce good was **exclusivity**, not land. ✅ Not all colonies were profitable; rule could be costly; profits were concentrated.

## 4. The economic driver

- ✅ Industrial powers wanted raw materials and markets; ✅ empire was tied to industrial capitalism; ✅ colonies were **reorganised for extraction**; ✅ the primary **structural** driver.
- ⚠️ Three distinct arguments: inputs and supply security; outlets under protectionism; capital export and its protection.
- ✅ **Hobson**, *Imperialism: A Study*, **1902** - under-consumption, surplus capital, financial capture of policy; the remedy is domestic redistribution, so imperialism is a **remediable policy**.
- ✅ **Lenin**, *Imperialism, the Highest Stage of Capitalism*, **written 1916, first published 1917** - monopoly, finance capital, capital export, the division of the world and redivision by war; a **necessary stage**.
- ✅ **Schumpeter** - imperialism as **atavism**, a survival of pre-capitalist military and aristocratic structures; capitalism erodes rather than produces it.
- ⚠️ Four counter-arguments: unprofitable colonies; flows went elsewhere; timing mismatch; other stated motives. ⚠️ Concede the flows; rebuild on supply security, concentrated interests and extractive structure.
- ⚠️ The decisive distinction: **economic motive** (poorly evidenced) against **economic structure** (well evidenced).
- ✅ The synthesis: economic motives were **central but not exclusive**.

## 5. The strategic driver

- ✅ **1869** Suez Canal opened - Africa and the Asian routes gain strategic weight. ✅ **1882** Britain occupies Egypt - strategic-financial imperialism becomes direct rule.
- ✅ Suez, sea routes and rival empires mattered; colonies were military-geopolitical assets; the driver explains **which territories were taken first**.
- ✅ Caution: **strategy often followed financial entanglement rather than preceding it**.
- ✅ The chain: canal 1869; unsustainable debt; purchase of the Khedive's shares 1875; European financial control; Egyptian nationalist and army opposition; British intervention and occupation 1882; French exclusion; a "temporary" occupation never ended.
- ⚠️ Three consequences: the Nile becomes a British requirement; France is humiliated and redirected; British freedom of manoeuvre in Europe is reduced.
- ✅ **1904** Entente Cordiale: France recognises Britain's position in Egypt for British approval of a possible French takeover of Morocco.
- ⚠️ The geometry: a British longitudinal axis crossing a French latitudinal one; they meet on the upper Nile.
- ⚠️ The formulation: Britain did not occupy Egypt in order to partition Africa, but having occupied Egypt could not avoid competing for Africa.

## 6. Prestige, domestic politics and the newcomers

- ✅ **1884** Bismarck backs German claims - the scramble becomes **openly competitive**. ✅ Recently unified Germany and Italy also sought colonies; ✅ empire became a **badge of great-power status**.
- ✅ The driver explains **timing and speed**; ✅ it cannot explain the economic reorganisation that followed.
- ⚠️ The rank argument: great-power status was a composite; colonies were the most quickly acquirable marker; the status return was high where the economic return was nil; newly created states had the strongest incentive.
- ✅ Italy - Eritrea and Somalia acquired; failure in Ethiopia at Adwa - the purest prestige case.
- ⚠️ Four explanations of Bismarck's turn: commercial protection; domestic politics; European diplomacy; the recognition race. Only the last compounds.
- ⚠️ Social imperialism: empire as a purchase of internal cohesion - a mechanism of political utility, untestable here.
- ⚠️ Five non-state actors: geographical societies; missions; trading and concession companies; consuls and officers; the press. ⚠️ The man-on-the-spot mechanism converts local initiative into national commitment.

## 7. Technology and medicine

- ✅ Steam transport, improved weapons and medicines increased European reach; conquest became easier. ✅ Technology **converts desire into feasibility** - the permissive cause. ✅ It explains **capability, never intent**.
- ⚠️ Four barriers and their removals: disease by prophylaxis; transport by steamer and railway; communication by telegraph; military parity by breech-loader, magazine rifle and machine gun.
- ⚠️ Arms-sale restriction maintained the gap by policy as well as by production - which is why Ethiopian acquisition mattered.
- ⚠️ Four limits: capability is not intent; the timing is imperfect; it explains battles rather than rule; it flatters the conqueror.
- ⚠️ Railways ran **producer-to-port**, not between African regions.

## 8. Ideology, race and the civilising mission

- ✅ Civilising-mission and racial-superiority rhetoric justified expansion; ✅ moral language often masked exploitation; ✅ ideology supplies **domestic legitimation** and shapes the **form of rule**; ✅ neither sincere motive nor pure cover - **it did real work in policy**.
- ✅ It was **not purely humanitarian**; it coexisted with racial superiority and extraction. ✅ Berlin was **not a humanitarian anti-slavery meeting alone**.
- ⚠️ Five components: commerce, Christianity, order, abolition, trusteeship - each authorising a policy.
- ⚠️ The scientific frame: racial hierarchy presented as classification; social Darwinism; and the decisive move of presenting hierarchy as a **finding** rather than a judgement.
- ✅ The method: **test stated motive against recorded conduct; that comparison is the analysis**.
- ✅ The core source's verdict: the real purpose was wealth and power; the coercive and exploitative nature **had to be disguised**; claimed benefits were **at best thinly spread**; officials generally treated subject peoples as inferior.
- ⚠️ The functional verdict: it enabled, it partially constrained by creating a public standard, and it decisively shaped the form of rule.

## 9. Ranking the causes

- ✅ A causes question tests **ranking and interaction**; ✅ a list of five motives is the answer to avoid; ✅ do not attribute imperialism to one factor only.
- ⚠️ Five causal types: structural (economic); directional (strategic); proximate (prestige and rivalry); permissive (technology); legitimating (ideology).
- ⚠️ The removal test: state what becomes inexplicable without each.
- ⚠️ Four couplings: financial entanglement into strategic commitment; cheapness into prestige acquisition; ideology into the description of extraction as improvement; territory as diplomatic currency. ⚠️ Only prestige plus a recognition rule compounds.
- ⚠️ Three defensible rankings by explanandum: occurrence favours economics; timing favours rivalry; territorial selection favours strategy.
- ✅ Verdict scaffold: **capitalism created the demand, rivalry set the pace, technology removed the obstacle, ideology dissolved the objection - no single one sufficient alone**.

## 10. The Congo and the road to Berlin

- ✅ The Congo Free State was recognised at Berlin under **Leopold II**; ✅ Berlin **legitimised a private-imperial venture** and **did nothing to prevent atrocity**; ✅ chartered or concessionary control shows **profit and sovereignty overlapping**.
- ✅ 1876 Brussels conference and the International African Association; Stanley's treaty campaign from about 1879; Brazza's rival French treaties; Portuguese claims at the mouth; the abandoned Anglo-Portuguese treaty of 1884; the Congo Free State from 1885; proprietorship of vacant land and produce; concession companies; collection quotas; armed enforcement; Morel's campaign and Casement's report of 1904; the Congo Reform Association of 1904; Belgian annexation in **1908**.
- ⚠️ Four conditions that made this basin the trigger: one navigable artery with one mouth; simultaneous interest of every power; a claimant weak enough for all to tolerate; a free-trade framing.
- ⚠️ Leopold's weakness was his asset: recognising him denied the basin to each power's strongest rival.
- ⚠️ The private-empire model: sovereignty and profit in one hand; revenue from compelled output; internal armed enforcement; no metropolitan scrutiny; a humanitarian charter that became the campaign's weapon.
- ✅ Belgian colonial method: advanced education **denied**; rivalries between groups used; **seventeen graduates** in the entire country at independence.
- ⚠️ **No casualty or population figure** for Congo Free State rule is held or asserted.

## 11. The Berlin Conference

- ✅ **15 November 1884 - 26 February 1885**; ✅ representatives from **fourteen** countries; ✅ **no African representation**; ✅ African voices did not shape the rules.
- ✅ The General Act recognised a framework for **effective occupation** and **accelerated later claims**. ✅ As adopted, the obligations applied to **new coastal acquisitions**, with **notification** and authority sufficient to protect existing rights; the doctrine that grew from it was wider.
- ✅ The Congo and Niger treated as **free waters**; ✅ freedom of trade in a conventional basin; ✅ anti-slave-trade declarations; ✅ recognition of Leopold's association.
- ⚠️ Every provision protects European interests against other Europeans; none creates an obligation enforceable by an African party.
- ✅ Three myths refuted: it did not partition Africa or draw borders; it did not start the scramble (Egypt 1882, German claims 1884, Leopold from about 1879); it was not a humanitarian anti-slavery conference.
- ✅ Thesis: **Berlin did not partition Africa; it standardised the terms on which partition could be recognised, and thereby accelerated a process already begun.** ✅ Verdict: **decisive as a legitimising rulebook and negligible as a cartographer.**
- ⚠️ The partition was completed without war among the European powers - a peace purchased by transferring the violence to Africa.

## 12. Execution and the partition pattern

- ⚠️ Five stages: paper claim; diplomatic claim; occupation; administration; boundary commission.
- ⚠️ The treaty form's three defects: capacity to sign; meaning of what was signed; a European audience.
- ✅ Royal Niger Company **1886**; Imperial British East Africa Company **1888**; British South Africa Company **1889** with **Cecil Rhodes**; German East Africa Company with **Carl Peters**.
- ⚠️ Columns were small forces of **African soldiers under European officers** - the conquest was carried out very largely by Africans in European service.
- ✅ Partition table: **Britain** - Egypt, Sudan, South Africa, Nigeria, East African holdings; **France** - much of West and Equatorial Africa, Algeria, Madagascar; **Belgium** - Congo; **Germany** - Togo, Cameroon, German East Africa, German South West Africa; **Portugal** - Angola, Mozambique; **Italy** - Eritrea, Somalia, and failure in Ethiopia.
- ⚠️ Shapes: Britain longitudinal and strategic; France a contiguous bloc large in area and small in value; Germany scattered and status-driven; Portugal coastal positions extended inland.
- ⚠️ The structural generalisation: the poorer the metropolitan power relative to its claim, the more it relied on compulsion.
- ✅ Portugal's rose-coloured map abandoned after the **British ultimatum of 1890**; the **Anglo-German agreement of 1890** settling East African boundaries and transferring **Heligoland**.
- ✅ Boundaries **rarely followed ethnic or political realities**; ⚠️ the mechanism is lines agreed between governments describing unvisited territory.
- ✅ The recorded flow: Berlin rules and effective occupation, then partition, then extraction, resistance and artificial borders.

## 13. The four flashpoints

- ✅ **Adwa, 1896** - Ethiopia defeats Italy; African resistance could succeed; conquest was contingent, not inevitable; ⚠️ exceptional. ✅ 1 March 1896; **Menelik II**; the disputed **Treaty of Wichale, 1889**; a treaty of 1896 recognising Ethiopian independence. ✅ Ethiopia was the only remaining independent African state in 1935, and the 1935 invasion was intended in part to avenge 1896.
- ⚠️ Four conditions for Adwa: unified command; modern firearms in quantity; favourable terrain and interior lines; an under-resourced adversary.
- ✅ **Fashoda, 1898** - Marchand in July; Kitchener after **Omdurman** on 2 September; French withdrawal in November; spheres fixed by declaration in 1899. ⚠️ The geometry: perpendicular axes meeting on the upper Nile.
- ✅ **South Africa, 1899-1902** - Britain defeats the Transvaal and the Orange Free State; ✅ the **Union of South Africa, 1910**, with about **70 per cent** black African, **18 per cent** white (about 60 per cent of them Dutch), **9 per cent** of mixed descent and **3 per cent** Asian; whites dominated politics and the economy; black people with few exceptions could not vote; most manual work in factories, gold mines and farms; male migrant workers in barracks away from their families. ✅ The core source records concentration camps in that war in which **about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died** - attributed; ⚠️ no figure is held for African camps.
- ✅ Mineral discoveries transformed the southern interior; **Cecil Rhodes** and the British South Africa Company; the failed **Jameson Raid, 1895-96**.
- ✅ **Morocco, 1905-06** - a German attempt to expand empire and test the Entente; Morocco one of the few remaining areas of Africa not under European control; **Algeciras, January 1906**; Britain, Russia, Italy and Spain supported France; a serious diplomatic defeat for Germany. ✅ **Agadir, 1911** - French occupation of **Fez**; the gunboat **Panther**; Lloyd George's Mansion House warning; France stood firm; Germany recognised the French protectorate for **two strips of the French Congo**.
- ⚠️ Boundary: the alliance system, the naval race and the July crisis belong to the 1914 topic.

## 14. African resistance

- ✅ Resistance was **frequent, though often defeated**; ✅ African agency must be recognised.
- ⚠️ Two types: resistance at conquest; revolt under colonial rule. The second is later and is evidence about administration.
- ✅ Cases: **Ethiopia at Adwa**; **Zulu** resistance; **Samori Ture**; ✅ the **Mahdist state** to Omdurman in 1898; the **Anglo-Zulu War of 1879**; Samori Ture's capture in **1898**.
- ✅ **Maji Maji, 1905-07**, against **labour and taxation demands**; ✅ the **genocide of the Herero and Nama, 1904-08**.
- ✅ Resistance was uneven because colonial powers had advantages in **logistics, weapons and divided local contexts**.
- ⚠️ Five failure mechanisms: the firepower gap maintained partly by arms-sale restriction; replaceable forces and logistics; divided local contexts and African allies; asymmetry of aims; sequential rather than simultaneous resistance.
- ⚠️ Adwa inverts four of the five.
- ⚠️ **No casualty figures** for any engagement or uprising.

## 15. Negotiation, intermediation and adaptation

- ✅ European rule depended on African **chiefs, soldiers, clerks, interpreters and tax collectors**, because the administrative presence was **thin**; ✅ this explains how tiny establishments governed vast territories and why some elites gained.
- ✅ **Collaboration is an analytical category, not a moral judgement**; ✅ adaptation was **constrained, not free choice**.
- ✅ Administration **recognised and sometimes invented chiefs**, hardening fluid identities into fixed administrative categories - ✅ a **direct mechanism** from colonial rule to later ethnic politics.
- ⚠️ Four strategic options for an African ruler: fight; seek the least dangerous protector; play rivals against each other; accept subordination for retained internal authority. ⚠️ Colonial terms varied with African bargaining strength.
- ✅ Four arenas of adaptation: cash crops; migrant labour; mission schooling; new legal forums - **used where possible**.
- ⚠️ Four-step route to later leadership: schooling; employment; experience of the gap between principle and practice; exclusion from higher posts. ✅ Confirmed negatively by the Belgian denial of advanced education.
- ✅ The formula: **Europe imposed, and African societies resisted, negotiated, adapted and suffered.**

## 16. The models of rule

- ✅ Mechanisms of control: effective occupation; river and trade rules; chartered or concessionary control; settler and administrative variation. ✅ **Different colonial powers ruled differently**; ✅ avoid writing that imperialism was **one uniform model**.
- ⚠️ Four models: direct rule and assimilation; indirect rule; concessionary rule; the settler colony. ✅ Indirect rule associated with **Lugard** and Northern Nigeria; ***The Dual Mandate in British Tropical Africa*, 1922**.
- ⚠️ Four determining variables: metropolitan fiscal capacity; pre-existing African political structure; climate and settlement viability; the economic character of the territory.
- ⚠️ Three domains: the model mattered decisively for political structure and for education and elite formation, and much less for the economic experience of most people.
- ✅ Belgian Congo: education denied; **seventeen graduates**. ✅ Nyasaland at withdrawal: **three secondary schools for three million people** and **not one industrial factory**.
- ✅ Kenya: the most fertile land, on the **highland plateau**, reserved for white settlers; a **colour bar**. ✅ Southern Rhodesia: settlers most firmly entrenched. ✅ West Africa, with no settler estates, saw a **comparatively straightforward** transfer of power.
- ⚠️ The verdict: the **settler/non-settler** axis did more work than the direct/indirect axis.
- ✅ Bounded India comparison: strategic routes, fiscal extraction, racial hierarchy, the language of improvement; ✅ **do not duplicate Indian material**.

## 17. The colonial political economy

- ✅ Colonies were **reorganised for extraction**.
- ⚠️ The engine: a **cash tax** on a largely cashless economy compels export cultivation or wage labour without any order to work being issued.
- ✅ Taxation in cash and labour obligations pushed men into **mines, plantations and porterage**, restructuring household economies and producing long-distance migration.
- ⚠️ Three land mechanisms: alienation to settlers; concession grants; the declaration of "vacant" land as state property - **expropriation through definition**.
- ⚠️ Four graded forms of compulsion: labour in lieu of tax; statutory labour obligations; penal contract and recruitment; quota compulsion.
- ✅ Male labour migration shifted **farming and household responsibility onto women**, while **colonial law and mission institutions redefined marriage and property** - the dimension most answers omit. ✅ Migrant workers in barracks away from their families; ⚠️ the rural household subsidised the wage.
- ✅ Conquest, forced labour, movement and disrupted food systems **raised mortality in several regions** - ✅ keep **qualitative**; no figures are sourced.
- ⚠️ Four structural features: producer-to-port transport; one or two commodities; no processing; an undeveloped internal market.
- ✅ Claimed benefits were **at best thinly spread**.
- ⚠️ Assert **no** tax, wage, acreage, output, mortality, population or living-standard figure.

## 18. Violence and the record

- ✅ Colonial rule **often meant coercion and atrocity**; ✅ the civilising mission was **deeply contradictory**.
- ⚠️ Four categories: conquest violence; administrative violence; extractive violence; exterminatory violence.
- ✅ The **genocide of the Herero and Nama, 1904-08** - ✅ name it precisely; ✅ do not generalise the term; ✅ give no casualty figures for it, for Maji Maji or for Congo Free State rule.
- ⚠️ Attribute to named regimes, administrations, commands, companies and policies - never to nations or peoples.
- ✅ Five defects of the record: administrator-made categories; African exclusion from its making; justificatory framing; partial recording of violence; African action as what survives to correct it. ✅ Where the record is quietest, look for what it was avoiding recording.
- ✅ The conclusion: **the colonial archive is usable but not neutral - it records European intention accurately, African society through European categories, and colonial violence only partially.**
- ✅ Control: do **not** name colonial officials, reports, ethnographies, archives or historiographical schools in support of that critique.

## 19. The inheritance

- ✅ Four long-term effects: **artificial borders**; **extractive economies** and commodity dependence; a hardened **racial order**; and **violent memory** from which anti-colonial nationalism drew strength.
- ✅ Boundaries rarely followed ethnic or political realities - **the single most cited long-term legacy**. ✅ New states were badly prepared; ✅ **neo-colonialism** left continuing dependence on western markets and investment; ✅ reliance on one or two commodities made price falls a major difficulty.
- ⚠️ The preservation argument: the inheritance; every alternative was worse; behavioural evidence; the cost.
- ✅ Evidence: the **OAU of 1963** as the forum for African conflicts; **neither the UN, the Commonwealth nor the OAU** would support **Biafra's** secession; **Katanga's** secession reversed with UN troops; **Singapore** left the Federation of Malaysia in **1965** by agreement.
- ✅ Cost: Nigeria's unity preserved only through a war in which **Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in the fighting**.
- ✅ Control: **do not claim a formal OAU border-inviolability resolution or cite a text for it**; state the mechanism, not a document.
- ✅ Verdict: **African states kept colonial borders not because they thought them just, but because a rule of revision would have had no stopping point - which is why the arbitrariness of 1885 became the permanent constitutional condition of the continent.**
- ⚠️ Anti-determinism: ✅ border inheritance is **not a deterministic explanation** for every later conflict.

## 20. The debates

- ✅ Four recorded syntheses: economic motives **central but not exclusive**; Berlin **accelerated and legitimated** rather than triggered; ideology as **both belief and instrument**; **distinguish types of control carefully**.
- ✅ **Hobson 1902**; ✅ **Lenin, written 1916 and first published 1917**; ✅ **Schumpeter** on atavism; ✅ **Gallagher and Robinson**, "The Imperialism of Free Trade", *Economic History Review*, **1953**, and *Africa and the Victorians*, **1961**.
- ⚠️ Each in four sentences: claim, argument, strongest objection, residual value. ⚠️ No theorist is declared correct; no statistic is attributed to any of them.
- ⚠️ Centre against periphery: metropolitan factors explain the disposition to expand; peripheral factors explain the timing and location of particular annexations.
- ✅ The Asian comparison: ✅ intervention in the crumbling Chinese Empire, where the European powers, the USA and Japan forced trading concessions, with the republic established in **1911**; ⚠️ formal partition in Africa against informal concessions in China.
- ⚠️ Counterfactual rule: admissible when it removes a single identified cause; inadmissible when it presupposes an institution, capability or knowledge that did not exist.

## 21. Answer architecture

- ✅ The syllabus clause: **colonization and its effect on society**; ✅ this topic sits at the core of that line.
- ✅ Demand map: causes test ranking and interaction; Berlin tests rules against conquest; consequences test land, labour, borders, authority, violence and health; agency tests **resistance and collaboration, adaptation and survival**; comparison tests direct rule against coastal influence; borders test **mechanism**, not determinism.
- ✅ Four thesis templates: multi-causal; Berlin; consequences; agency.
- ✅ Mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20 marks; ⚠️ word ceilings 150 and 250; ⚠️ compress by evidence at 20 marks, not by omission.
- ✅ Three verdict scaffolds plus the border-preservation verdict.
- ⚠️ Five hand-offs: industrialisation; nationalism and the unifications; the world in 1914; decolonisation; colonial India.

## 22. Rapid recall - the dates that do argumentative work

| Date | Item | The argument it serves |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ 1795 / 1814 | Cape captured / confirmed British | The southern strategic axis predates the canal |
| ✅ 1835-40 | Great Trek; Boer migration northwards | The origin of the inland Boer communities |
| ✅ 1852 / 1854 | Transvaal recognised (Sand River); Orange Free State recognised (Bloemfontein) | The settler republics of 1899-1902 |
| ✅ 1869 | Suez Canal opened | Strategy becomes directional |
| ✅ 1870s | About 10 per cent; control mainly coastal | The baseline |
| ✅ 1882 | Britain occupies Egypt | Financial entanglement becomes occupation |
| ✅ 1884 | Bismarck backs German claims | The scramble becomes openly competitive |
| ✅ 1884-85 | Berlin Conference, 15 Nov - 26 Feb; fourteen countries | The recognition rule |
| ✅ 1885 | Congo Free State recognised under Leopold II | Sovereignty and profit in one hand |
| ✅ 1896 | Adwa | Conquest was contingent |
| ✅ 1898 | Fashoda; Omdurman | The procedural peace at its limit |
| ✅ 1899-1902 | The Boer War | Empire was not cheap |
| ✅ 1904 | Entente Cordiale | Egypt traded against Morocco |
| ✅ 1904-08 | Genocide of the Herero and Nama | The civilising mission refuted by conduct |
| ✅ 1905-06 / 1911 | Morocco; Algeciras; Agadir | African territory as a European counter |
| ✅ 1905-07 | Maji Maji | Revolt against administration, not invasion |
| ✅ 1908 | Belgian annexation of the Congo | Concession collapses into state rule |
| ✅ 1910 | Union of South Africa | A settlement over an excluded majority |
| ✅ 1914 | About 90 per cent; Ethiopia and Liberia | The benchmark |
| ✅ 1963 | OAU founded | The border-preservation evidence |

## 23. Prohibited formulations

- ✅ "The Berlin Conference partitioned Africa" / "drew the borders".
- ✅ Any participant figure other than **fourteen**; any dates other than **15 November 1884 - 26 February 1885**.
- ✅ Any variation of the **10 per cent / 90 per cent** figures, or their use without attribution.
- ✅ "Africans did not resist."
- ✅ "All colonies were profitable."
- ✅ Any casualty figure for **Maji Maji**, the **Herero and Nama genocide** or **Congo Free State rule**.
- ⚠️ "Colonial genocide in Africa" used generally, instead of the genocide of the Herero and Nama.
- ⚠️ Atrocity attributed to a nation or a people.
- ✅ Indian colonial figures imported into this topic.
- ✅ Named colonial officials, reports, ethnographies, archives or historiographical schools used to support the source critique.
- ✅ A formal **OAU border-inviolability resolution**, or any text or article cited for it.
- ✅ Improvised quantitative claims about the **Atlantic slave trade** or abolition.
- ⚠️ Any tax, wage, acreage, output, mortality, population or living-standard figure.
- ⚠️ Any statistic attributed to Hobson, Lenin, Schumpeter or Gallagher-Robinson, or any claim that one of them was shown to be correct.
- ⚠️ Any claim that Egypt after 1882 was a British colony.
- ⚠️ Any claim that the Congo Free State was a Belgian colony before 1908.

## Construction record for this edition

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| Lesson count | Derived from the topic's own dependency chain and both owners rather than from a template; twenty-one lessons, with the five driver families taught separately before being ranked, the conference taught after the crisis that produced it, and African action given two lessons rather than one so that resistance and intermediation are both evidenced |
| Compiled four-option MCQ corpus | Not constructed, in accordance with the concept-check rule for this generation; one answer-free concept check, model answer and misconception note per lesson, plus ten cumulative checks across five blocks and sixty corrective drills |
| Workbook operations | None performed; no MCQ corpus was created, copied or relocated, and no solved-PYQ workbook was generated or migrated |
| Solved PYQ model answers | Not included; the topic owns no verified question, and every adjacent objective item is recorded answer-neutrally with no key asserted or inferred |
| Bounded History research rule | Applied. The substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and one core OCR history source. External work was confined to exact PYQ verification and routing, one bounded material-fact pass for standard essentials absent from the owners, and one current linkage. Exactly one linkage is carried, in Lesson 19; the other twenty checklists record truthfully that no current affairs were sought. No per-lesson searches and no optional corroboration were performed |
| Bounded material-fact pass | One pass, conducted because the two owners supply a nine-row timeline, a driver table, a partition table and an answer architecture, and deliberately do not narrate the connecting events. ## Book evidence and its controls

1. `books\WORLD HISTORY -- NORMAN LOWE -- ENGLISH  ##.pdf`, read through the locally held extracted text at `attempts\_gs_source_text\WORLD_HISTORY_NORMAN_LOWE_ENGLISH_pdf.txt` - the folder's designated world-history spine and the **one core OCR history source** used for this topic under the bounded History research rule. Its complete text was searched term by term. It supplied: the definition of imperialism as the building up of an empire by seizing territory overseas, the statement that the European powers took part in a great burst of imperialist expansion in the years after 1880, and the record that most of Africa was taken over in what became known as the Scramble for Africa, the idea behind it being mainly to get control of new markets and new sources of raw materials; the reference to the Berlin Conference of 1885 as the date used a century later by a Congolese nationality law restricting citizenship to those who could demonstrate a family connection with the Congo at that time; the record that Abyssinia was the only remaining independent state left in Africa in 1935 and that Italian involvement went back to 1896, when an Italian attempt to colonise it had ended in defeat at Adowa; its map of Africa and the peace treaties, which marks Fashoda and records the colonial pattern of 1914 and the transfer of German colonies as mandates in 1919; the Moroccan Crisis of 1905-06 with the Entente Cordiale understanding on Egypt and Morocco, Morocco as one of the few remaining areas of Africa not controlled by a European power, the Algeciras conference of January 1906 and the German diplomatic defeat; the Agadir Crisis of 1911 with the occupation of Fez, the gunboat Panther, the Mansion House warning, and German recognition of the French protectorate in return for two strips of the French Congo; the southern African sequence from the capture of the Cape in 1795 and the settlement of 1814 through emancipation in 1838, the Great Trek of 1835-40 and the Transvaal and Orange Free State - which that source brackets together as 1835-40, and which this session separates under the disputed-facts record above - to the Boer War of 1899-1902 and the Union of South Africa of 1910, together with the Union's recorded racial composition, its political exclusion of black voters, the concentration of manual work in factories, gold mines and farms, and the barracks accommodation of male migrant workers; the verdict passage on decolonisation and imperialism, including the judgement that the empire's real purpose was to increase wealth and power and that its coercive and exploitative nature had to be disguised, the assessments of empire it quotes from four named writers, the record that claimed benefits were at best thinly spread, the statement that most officials treated subject peoples as inferior, the record that retribution was usually swift and disproportionate wherever there was resistance, and the record of concentration camps during the Boer War in which about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died; the record of artificial frontiers forced on new states, neo-colonial dependence on western markets and investment, and reliance on exports of only one or two commodities; the account of Belgian policy in the Congo denying Africans advanced education and using rivalries between groups, with only seventeen graduates in the entire country at independence and no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or officers; the record that Nyasaland had only three secondary schools for three million Africans and not one industrial factory at British withdrawal; the settler-factor accounts for Kenya, including the reservation of the fertile highland plateau for white settlers and the colour bar, and for Southern Rhodesia; the note that West Africa, without permanent settler estates to defend, saw a comparatively straightforward move to independence; and the record of intervention in the crumbling Chinese Empire by the European powers, the USA and Japan, with the republic established in 1911.
2. **Control result for the nineteenth-century core.** The same extracted text was searched for every central term of this topic. The zero-returns are recorded as evidence of a check performed, and the items listed above are the entirety of what the book legitimately supplies.
3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded below as open uncertainty rather than filled from outside.

## The single bounded material-fact verification pass

⚠️ **Why the pass was necessary.** The two owners supply a nine-row timeline, a driver table, a partition table, agency and consequence evidence banks and a complete answer architecture, and they deliberately do not narrate the connecting events. Several of those events are standard essentials without which the teaching would have to use vague formulations - "a king obtained a claim", "a crisis occurred in Egypt", "two powers met on a river" - where the standard account has exact names, dates and instruments. The instruction for this pass was explicit that essential standard coverage must not be suppressed and that names must not be withheld. One bounded pass was therefore run, confined to those essentials, and every item it produced is labelled in the lesson that uses it.

| Cluster verified | Items carried |
|---|---|
| African polities and rulers | The Ethiopian empire under Menelik II; the Sokoto Caliphate; Asante; Dahomey; Samori Ture's Wassoulou empire, its roughly two decades of resistance to French expansion from the 1880s and his capture in 1898; the Zulu kingdom and the Anglo-Zulu War of 1879 with its severe early British defeat; the Mahdist state governing Sudan from the mid-1880s until Omdurman in September 1898; Buganda; the Sultanate of Zanzibar |
| The Egyptian chain | The canal built under a concession with heavy Egyptian borrowing; unsustainable debt; the British purchase of the Khedive's canal shares in 1875; Anglo-French financial supervision; Egyptian nationalist and army opposition; British military intervention and occupation in 1882; French non-participation and exclusion; a veiled occupation under retained Khedivial and Ottoman forms |
| The Congo sequence | The Brussels geographical conference of 1876 and the International African Association; Stanley's station-building and treaty campaign from about 1879; Brazza's rival French treaties on the northern bank; Portuguese historic claims at the river mouth; the abandoned Anglo-Portuguese treaty of 1884; the Congo Free State from 1885 with Leopold II as sovereign in a personal capacity; proprietorship over vacant land and its produce; concession companies; collection quotas; armed enforcement; E. D. Morel's campaign; Roger Casement's consular report of 1904; the Congo Reform Association of 1904; Belgian annexation in 1908 |
| The Berlin instrument | Convening at Berlin on the German chancellor's initiative in conjunction with France; participation including the principal European powers, the Ottoman Empire and the United States; the General Act signed on 26 February 1885; the occupation obligations as adopted applying to new acquisitions on the coasts, with notification to the other signatories and authority sufficient to protect existing rights; freedom of trade in a defined conventional Congo basin without differential duties |
| Chartered companies and conversion | The Royal Niger Company chartered in 1886; the Imperial British East Africa Company in 1888; the British South Africa Company in 1889 associated with Cecil Rhodes; the German East Africa Company associated with Carl Peters; standardised European-language treaty forms explained through interested interpreters; columns of locally recruited African soldiers under European officers; boundary conventions described by coordinates, watersheds or rivers and surveyed later by joint commissions |
| European collisions | Portugal's rose-coloured map abandoned after the British ultimatum of 1890; the Anglo-German agreement of 1890 settling East African boundaries, transferring Heligoland and recognising Britain's position in Zanzibar; the failed Jameson Raid of 1895-96; major mineral discoveries transforming the southern African interior; the Sand River Convention of 1852 recognising the Transvaal, or South African Republic, and the Bloemfontein Convention of 1854 recognising the Orange Free State; Adwa fought on 1 March 1896 with the Treaty of Wichale of 1889 as its origin and a treaty of 1896 recognising Ethiopian independence; Marchand's occupation of Fashoda in July 1898, Kitchener's arrival after Omdurman on 2 September 1898, the French withdrawal in November 1898 and the Anglo-French declaration of 1899 |
| Enabling technologies | Quinine used prophylactically from the middle decades of the nineteenth century; the shallow-draught iron river steamer and the colonial railway; submarine cables and land telegraph lines; breech-loading rifles, magazine rifles and the early machine gun; European restriction of modern firearms sales to African states |
| Administration and doctrine | Frederick Lugard and Northern Nigeria as the model of indirect rule; *The Dual Mandate in British Tropical Africa*, published in 1922, as its classic statement; hut, poll and head taxes assessed per dwelling or per adult male and collected through chiefs; statutory labour obligations, penal-sanctioned contract labour and porterage |
| Theorists of imperialism | John Atkinson Hobson, *Imperialism: A Study*, 1902, and its under-consumption argument with domestic redistribution as its remedy; Vladimir Ilyich Lenin, *Imperialism, the Highest Stage of Capitalism*, written 1916 and first published 1917, and its monopoly, finance-capital and redivision argument; Joseph Schumpeter's atavism thesis; John Gallagher and Ronald Robinson, "The Imperialism of Free Trade", *Economic History Review*, 1953, and *Africa and the Victorians*, 1961, with the formal/informal distinction and the peripheral or excentric explanation |
| Background and context | Trans-Saharan caravan trade; the Sudanic Islamic states; the Indian Ocean and Swahili commercial world with Zanzibar as its principal entrepot; the Atlantic system, its abolition and the growth of legitimate commerce, carried without any quantity; Dutch settlement at the Cape from the seventeenth century and French Algeria from 1830 as pre-period European holdings; the extended later-nineteenth-century period of depressed prices and the European return to protective tariffs; nineteenth-century Sudanic and southern African state-building; Ethiopian imperial reconstruction; Egyptian state modernisation; racial hierarchy presented as scientific classification and social Darwinism |

⚠️ **Control on the pass.** No item from the pass overrides an owner. Where an owner and the pass address the same point, the owner's formulation is used and the pass supplies only the missing precision. Where the pass produced material outside the topic's period or boundary, it was not used. No item from the pass carries a quantitative, archival or casualty claim. The pass was run once and is not reopened.

## Open uncertainty recorded rather than filled

1. **Magnitudes.** The human cost of Congo Free State rule, of the genocide of the Herero and Nama, of Maji Maji and of every campaign in this topic is not available in the material held here and is not asserted. The direction and character of the violence are recorded; its scale is recorded as unavailable.
2. **Comparative causal weight.** Whether any single ranking of the five drivers holds across the whole continent cannot be demonstrated from this material; the rankings in Lesson 9 are argued by explanandum rather than measured.
3. **The chronology of effective administration.** When particular territories passed from claim to working colonial government is not held here, so the gap between the map and the ground is asserted as a structural feature with named illustrations rather than dated.
4. **Internal decision-making.** No diplomatic, cabinet, company or campaign records are held; motives are inferred from pattern of action and from recorded characterisations, and the internal history of the Berlin Conference in particular is recorded as beyond this material.
5. **African testimony.** None is held. The correction applied throughout is the one the standard account prescribes: recorded African action is used as the counter-source.
6. **The threshold year.** The owners locate the turn variously at 1869, "after 1880" and 1884; no document here fixes a single starting date, and the 1880s are used as the turn with 1884-85 as the point of formalisation.
7. **The Atlantic trade.** The standard account records this as a bounded gap in its own coverage; this session states the structural point and asserts no quantity.
8. **Economic convergence between models of rule.** The claim in Lesson 16 that economic experience converged across administrative models is argued from identical structural requirements rather than demonstrated from comparative data, and is recorded as open.
